Convergence rate to equilibrium for conservative scattering models on the torus: a new tauberian approach
B. Lods, M. Mokhtar-Kharroubi

Abstract
. The object of this paper is to provide a new and systematic tauberian approach to quantitative long time behaviour of C 0 C_{0} -semigroups ( 𝒱 ⁡ ( t ) ) t ⩾ 0 \left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0} in L 1 ​ ( 𝕋 d × ℝ d ) L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}) governing conservative linear kinetic equations on the torus with general scattering kernel 𝒌 ⁡ ( v , v ′ ) \bm{k}(v,v^{\prime}) and degenerate (i.e. not bounded away from zero) collision frequency σ ⁡ ( v ) = ∫ ℝ d 𝒌 ⁡ ( v ′ , v ) ​ 𝒎 ​ ( d ​ v ′ ) \sigma(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{k}(v^{\prime},v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}) , (with 𝒎 ⁡ ( d ​ v ) \bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v) being absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure). We show in particular that if N 0 N_{0} is the maximal integer s ⩾ 0 s\geqslant 0 such that 1 σ ( ⋅ ) ∫ ℝ d 𝒌 ( ⋅ , v ) σ − s ( v ) 𝒎 ( d v ) ∈ L ∞ ( ℝ d ) \frac{1}{\sigma(\cdot)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{k}(\cdot,v)\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}) then, for initial datum f f such that ∫ 𝕋 d × ℝ d | f ⁡ ( x , v ) | ​ σ − N 0 ​ ( v ) ​ 𝑑 x ​ 𝒎 ​ ( 𝑑 v ) < ∞ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(x,v)|\sigma^{-N_{0}}(v)\mathrm{d}x\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)<\infty it holds ‖ 𝒱 ⁡ ( t ) ​ f − ϱ f ​ Ψ ‖ L 1 ​ ( 𝕋 d × ℝ d ) = ε f ​ ( t ) ( 1 + t ) N 0 − 1 , ϱ f := ∫ ℝ d f ⁡ ( x , v ) ​ 𝑑 x ​ 𝒎 ​ ( 𝑑 v ) \left\|\mathcal{V}(t)f-\varrho_{f}\Psi\right\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\dfrac{{\varepsilon}_{f}(t)}{(1+t)^{N_{0}-1}},\qquad\varrho_{f}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v) where Ψ \Psi is the unique invariant density of ( 𝒱 ⁡ ( t ) ) t ⩾ 0 \left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0} and lim t → ∞ ε f ​ ( t ) = 0 \lim_{t\to\infty}{\varepsilon}_{f}(t)=0 . We in particular provide a new criteria of the existence of invariant density. The proof relies on the explicit computation of the time decay of each term of the Dyson-Phillips expansion of ( 𝒱 ⁡ ( t ) ) t ⩾ 0 \left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0} and on suitable smoothness and integrability properties of the trace on the imaginary axis of Laplace transform of remainders of large order of this Dyson-Phillips expansion. Our construction resorts also on collective compactness arguments and provides various technical results of independent interest. Finally, as a by-product of our analysis, we derive essentially sharp “subgeometric” convergence rate for Markov semigroups associated to general transition kernels. MSC: primary 82C40; secondary 35F15, 47D06 Keywords: Kinetic equation; Markov semigroups; Convergence to equilibrium; Dyson-Phillips expansion; Inverse Laplace transform.



1.
Introduction

The objet of this paper is to provide
L^{1}
–rates of convergence to equilibrium for
conservative linear kinetic equations of the form

(1.1)

\partial_{t}f(x,v,t)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(x,v,t)+\sigma(x,v)f(x,v,t)=\int_{V}k(x,v,v^{\prime})f(t,x,v^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}),

for
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V,
and
t\geqslant 0
where

\sigma(x,v)=\int_{V}k(x,v^{\prime},v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}),\qquad(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V.

Here
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the support of a nonnegative Borel measure
\bm{m}
while
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional torus

\mathbb{T}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\big/\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

For simplicity, we will assume that the Lebesgue measure on the torus is normalized i.e.
|\mathbb{T}^{d}|=1
.

1.1.
Assumptions and main result

This class of equation was dealt with in
[
34
,
36
]
for a general class of velocity measures
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
. A key result in
[
34
]
is that the semigroup governing (
1.1
) has a spectral gap if and only if

\lim_{t\to\infty}\inf_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(x+tv,v)\mathrm{d}t>0.

In this case, there exists automatically an invariant density and the latter is exponentially stable (i.e. the semigroup converges exponentially, in operator norm, to the spectral projection associated to the invariant density). The existence and the stability of an invariant density in the degenerate case

(1.2)

\lim_{t\to\infty}\inf_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(x+tv,v)\mathrm{d}t=0

are dealt with systematically in
[
36
]
. The stability of the invariant density (i.e. the strong convergence of the semigroup to its ergodic projection) is
not quantified
and is obtained either by means of general results on partially integral semigroups
[
39
]
or by means of a
0-1
law for semigroups
[
35
]
. We provide also in Remark
6.4
below a third approach via Ingham tauberian theorem.

Our object here, in a continuation of
[
36
]
, is to provide rates of convergence to equilibrium in the spirit of our recent construction on collisionless kinetic semigroups with boundary operators
[
27
]
. To this end, we restrict ourselves to space homogeneous scattering kernel

k(x,v,v^{\prime})=\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})

(and consequently
\sigma(x,v)=\sigma(v)
) where the degeneracy condition (
1.2
) amounts to

(1.3)

\inf_{v\in V}\sigma(v)=0.

The non homogeneous case is left open even if we suspect that a similar, albeit much more technical, construction is possible in that case. We also assume that

(1.4)

\sigma\in L^{\infty}(V)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sigma(v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v^{\prime},v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}),\qquad v\in V.

We will see in Assumption
1.5
that we willrestrict ourselves to the case in which
\bm{m}
is
absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure

\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\bm{m}(v)\mathrm{d}v

for some nonnegative weight function
\bm{m}\>:\>V\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfying some technical regularity assumption (see (
1.13
) for details). The specific nature of the Lebesgue measure is used only once (in the proof of Lemma
3.8
) and could be avoided at the cost of more technical calculations. We have not tried to elaborate on this point here because the whole construction given in the paper is already quite involved. We however insist on the fact that the choice of the Lebesgue measure seems to be only technical.
We denote by

\mathbb{X}_{0}:=L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V\,,\,\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v))

endowed with its usual norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}.
More generally, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

\mathbb{X}_{s}:=L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V\,,\,\max(1,\sigma(v)^{-s})\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v))

with norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}.

Notice that the absorption semigroup
\left(U_{0}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
given by

(1.5)

U_{0}(t)f(x,v)=\exp\left(-\sigma(v)\,t\right)f(x-tv,v),\qquad t\geqslant 0,f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

has zero type in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
:

\omega(U_{0})=0

under the degeneracy condition (
1.3
).
The generator of
\left(U_{0}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is given by

\mathcal{A}f(x,v)=-v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(x,v)-\sigma(v)f(x,v),\qquad f\in\mathscr{D}(\mathcal{A})=\left\{f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\;;\;v\cdot\nabla_{x}f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\right\}.

We introduce the operator, acting in the
v
-variable only,

(1.6)

\mathcal{K}f(v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})f(v^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}),\qquad f\in L^{1}(V)=L^{1}(V,\mathrm{d}\bm{m}).

Due to (
1.4
), one sees first that

\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0}),\qquad\qquad\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}

and also that

\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0}),\qquad\qquad\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}=1.

The fact that
\mathcal{K}
is a bounded operator in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
implies that
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}
is the generator of a
C_{0}
-semigroup
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
given by

\mathcal{V}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}U_{n}(t),\qquad t\geqslant 0

where

U_{n+1}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{n}(t-s)\mathcal{K}U_{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}U_{0}(t-s)\mathcal{K}U_{n}(t-s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},\qquad t\geqslant 0.

Introduce the following notation, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}

(1.7)

\vartheta_{s}(w):=\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{V}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v),\qquad\qquad w\in V.

The results of the present paper are based upon several sets of Assumptions. The first
fundamental assumptions
are the following which are at the basis of the underlying method:

Assumption 1.1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{K}\>:\>L^{1}(V)\to L^{1}(V)
is a
weakly compact
operator of the form (
1.6
) which satisfies the following

(1)

For any
v\in V

(1.8)

\sigma(v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(w,v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w),

with
\sigma\in L^{\infty}(V)
and

(1.9)

\inf_{v}\sigma(v)=0.

(2)

There exists some (maximal) integer
N_{0}\geqslant 1
such that

(1.10)

\vartheta_{N_{0}}\in L^{\infty}(V).

(3)

Introducing, for any
\delta>0
the set

\Sigma_{\delta}=\{v\in V\;;\;\sigma(v)\leqslant\delta\},

we assume that

(1.11)

\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\sup_{w\in V}\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{\Sigma_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=0\,

These assumptions provide actually a new practical criteria ensuring the existence (and uniqueness) of an invariant density:

Theorem 1.2
.

Assume that
\mathcal{K}
is an irreducible operator satisfying Assumptions
1.1
and the measure
\bm{m}
is such that that there exists
\alpha>0
such that, for any bounded set
S\subset V
, there is
c(S)>0
such that

(1.12)

\sup_{\nu\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\bm{m}\otimes\bm{m}\big(\left\{(v,w)\in S\times S\;;\;|(v-w)\cdot\nu|<\varepsilon\right\}\big)\leqslant c(S)\varepsilon^{\alpha},\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0.

Then, there exists a unique
{\Psi}\in\mathscr{D}(\mathcal{A})

spatially homogeneous
with

\Psi(v)>0,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\Psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=1

such that

\left(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}\right)\Psi=0=\mathcal{K}\Psi.

Moreover,
\Psi\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}-1}
.

Remark 1.3
.

We recall that
\mathcal{K}
is irreducible if there exists no non trivial
\Omega\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
such that
\mathcal{K}
leaves invariant
L^{1}(\Omega)
which is identified to the closed subspace of
\mathbb{X}_{0}
of functions vanishing a.e. outside
\Omega
. Practical criterion ensuring the irreducibility of
\mathcal{K}
is given in
[
36
, Proposition 7]
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}
is irreducible if
\bm{k}(v,w)>0
for
\bm{m}\otimes\bm{m}
-a.e.
(v,w)\in V\times V
. Notice that the existence and uniqueness of a steady solution has been obtained, under
different assumptions
in
[
36
]
. The approach followed here is technically different from
[
36
]
and, as said, resort to different assumptions (see Proposition
5.1
for details).

Remark 1.4
.

Notice that, if
\bm{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
satisfies a
detailed balance
condition, i.e. there exists a positive spatially homogeneous density
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}(v)
,
\mathcal{M}\in L^{1}(V)
such that

\bm{k}(v,w)\mathcal{M}(w)=\mathcal{M}(v)\bm{k}(v,w),\qquad\forall v,w\in V

then, up to a normalisation factor,
\Psi=\mathcal{M}
is an invariant density and assumption (
1.11
) is not needed for our analysis. Of course, assumption (
1.12
) is satisfied if
\bm{m}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is the framework we will further adopt in the paper.

A second set of Assumptions, most of technical nature, is the following

Assumption 1.5
.

The measure
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure

\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\bm{m}(v)\mathrm{d}v

for some weight function
\bm{m}
such that

(1.13)

\sup_{v\in V}\left|v\cdot\nabla_{v}\log\bm{m}(v)\right|<\infty.

Moreover, the kernel
\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})
is such that there exist two positive constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

(1.14)

\int_{V}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(v,w)\right|\max\left(1,\sigma^{-1}(v)\right)\bm{m}(v)\mathrm{d}v\leqslant C_{1}\sigma(w)\qquad\forall w\in V

and

(1.15)

\int_{V}\left|v\cdot\nabla_{v}\bm{k}(v,w)\right|\bm{m}(v)\mathrm{d}v\leqslant C_{2}\sigma({w})\qquad\forall{w}\in V.

Remark 1.6
.

We will comment in Subsection
1.2
below on this set of assumptions as well as to the subsequent Assumption
1.1
. We only mention here that property (
1.13
) is satisfied for instance for weight functions of the form

\bm{m}(v)=\left(1+|v|^{2}\right)^{\frac{s}{2}},\qquad s\geqslant 0.

Our main result is the following

Theorem 1.7
.

Under Assumptions
1.1
and
1.5
, if
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is an irreducible semigroup then for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
there exist a constant
C_{f}>0
and

\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}\in\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})

such that

(1.16)

\left\|\mathcal{V}(t)f-\varrho_{f}\Psi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{C_{f}}{(1+t)^{N_{0}-1}}\bm{\varepsilon}(t)\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0,

where
\varrho_{f}:=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}f(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
and

(1.17)

\bm{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{1}{1+t}+\left\|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta\,t\right)\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\,,\,\qquad\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\varepsilon}(t)=0.

Moreover, for any
\mathsf{p}>4
,
there is some positive constant
K=K(\mathsf{p})>0
such that

(1.18)

\left\|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta\,t\right)\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant K\,\left(\omega_{f}\left(\frac{\pi}{t}\right)\right)^{\frac{\mathsf{p}-4}{\mathsf{p}}}\qquad\forall t\geqslant 1

where
\omega_{f}\>:\>\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
denotes the
minimal modulus of uniform continuity
of the mapping
\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}
.

Remark 1.8
.

Notice that
(\mathcal{V}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
is irreducible if there is no invariant subspace
L^{1}(\Omega)
of
\mathbb{X}_{0}
left invariant by
\mathcal{V}(t)
for any
t\geqslant 0.
One can prove that if
\mathcal{K}
is irreducible then so is
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
(see
[
36
, Proposition 7]
).

Remark 1.9
.

Recall that, in Assumption
1.1
, we assumed
N_{0}\geqslant 1
to be an integer. Without such an assumption, i.e if
N_{0}=\lfloor N_{0}\rfloor+\alpha
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, our main decay rate will then read

\left\|\mathcal{V}(t)f-\varrho_{f}\Psi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{C_{f}}{(1+t)^{\lfloor N_{0}\rfloor-1}}\bm{\varepsilon}(t)\qquad\quad f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}.

In that case, we believe that, for concrete examples of collision kernel
\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})
, it should be possible to explicit
\bm{\varepsilon}(t)
through an identification of the modulus of continuity of
\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}
in terms of the non-integer part
\alpha=N_{0}-\lfloor N_{0}]\rfloor.

Here above and in all the sequel, for any Banach space
(X,\|\cdot\|_{X})
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},X)=\left\{h\>:\>\mathbb{R}\to X\;;\;\text{of class $\mathscr{C}^{k}$ over $\mathbb{R}$ }\right.\\
\left.\text{and such that }\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{j}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{j}}h(\eta)\right\|_{X}=0\qquad\forall j\leqslant k\right\}

and we endow
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},X)
with the norm

\|h\|_{\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},X)}:=\max_{0\leqslant j\leqslant k}\sup_{\eta}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{j}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{j}}h(\eta)\right\|_{X}

which makes it a Banach space. We of course adopt the notation
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},X)=\mathscr{C}_{0}^{0}(\mathbb{R},X)
.

The above main result of the paper provides an explicit decay of the solution to (
1.1
). We strongly believe however that the interest of the present paper goes far beyond the mere convergence rate but it paves the way to a general abstract tauberian approach to the convergence rate of perturbed stochastic semigroup
[
28
]
. Moreover, because of the use of several fine collective compactness results and decay of Dyson-Phillips iterates, the paper contains several intermediate results of fundamental interest.

We can already mention that the function
\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)
appearing in (
1.18
) is related to suitable derivatives of the trace along the imaginary axis
\lambda=i\eta

(\eta\in\mathbb{R})
of the Laplace transform of suitable (large order) reminder of the Dyson-Phillips series defining the semigroup
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. See Theorem
2.6
and its proof in Section
2
for a more precise statement.

It is important to mention that, as a direct by-product of our construction, our analysis covers also the important case in which the initial datum
f_{0}
is independent of
x
. In that case, the general solution
f(x,v,t)=f(v,t)
is also independent of
x
and satisfies the
spatially homogenous
version of (
1.1
) which can be rewritten as

(1.19)

\partial_{t}f(v,t)=\int_{V}\left[\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})f(t,v^{\prime})-\bm{k}(v^{\prime},v)f(t,v)\right]\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime})\,.

Models governed by eq. (
1.19
) are ubiquitous in the study of Markov processes and results like our main Theorem
1.7
provide in this case an estimate of the decay rate for the transition probability semigroup of a continuous time Markov jump process. For such jump processes, roughly speaking, positive lower bound on the transition kernel
\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})
induces exponential convergence of the stochastic processes (this case is refer to as the “geometric case” in the study of Markov processes) whereas our degeneracy condition (
1.9
) prevents such an exponential convergence. Our result provides therefore a (seemingly sharp) “subgeometric” convergence rate for these kinds of processes. Our construction is quite involved and relies on various technical results of independent interest. As far as we know, most of our results are new and appear here for the first time.

1.2.
About Assumptions
1.1
and
1.5

Let us comment a bit about Assumptions
1.1
–
1.5
, referring the reader to the subsequent Section
2
for more details on that matter. We first observe that the conservative assumption (
1.8
) is natural to deal with Markov semigroups whereas, as said already, the degeneracy condition (
1.9
) is the one which prevents the existence of a spectral gap.

As illustrated by the above Theorem
1.7
, the decay rate is
prescribed
by the maximal gain of integrability that the boundary operator is able to provide through the function
\vartheta_{N_{0}}
where we notice that

(1.20)

\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}),\qquad\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}})}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{N_{0}}\|_{\infty}.

This illustrates the fundamental role of (
1.10
) in Assumption
1.1
. The fact that we assume here
N_{0}
to be an integer is an artefact of the approach we follow since, as established in Theorem
2.6
,
N_{0}-1
is also the maximal regularity of the trace function
\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f
we can derive for
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
.

We already pointed out that a consequence of Assumptions
1.1
concerns the existence of an invariant density
\Psi
in Theorem
1.2
since it allows to apply the results from
[
36
]
. Moreover, (
1.11
) is the cornerstone hypothesis to establish that there exists
\mathsf{q}>0
such that

(1.21)

\left\{\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{\mathsf{q}}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})\text{ is collectively compact}

in Theorem
2.4
.
All these consequences of Assumptions
1.1
are the fundamental brick on which we build our theory. The role of Assumptions
1.5
, on the contrary, is more of technical nature. Indeed, Assumptions
1.5
are technical requirements which allow to deduce the decay rate of iterates of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
on the imaginary axis with respect to
|\mathrm{Im}\lambda\,|
. We refer to Theorem
2.4
and especially (
2.11
) for a precise statement. In particular, under such an assumption, we point out (see (
2.12
)) that

\int_{|\eta|>1}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{\mathsf{p}}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty

for any
\mathsf{p}>4
which is crucial for the estimate (
1.18
).

We wish to insist here on the fact that the assumption (
1.11
) is the one ensuring the above collective compactness (
1.21
) whereas (
1.14
)–(
1.13
) (together with the fact that
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is absolutely continuous w. r. t. the Lebesgue measure) are those assumptions which provide
quantitative estimates
on the behaviour of iterates of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
. In particular, all the results of the paper which resort only on some
qualitative
properties of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
remain valid
without the assumptions (
1.14
)–(
1.13
)
. See for instance Theorem
6.3
and Remark
6.4
for an example of such qualitative results.

1.3.
Related literature

A very precise exposition of modern tools developed for the convergence to equilibrium and stability of Markov processes is the monograph
[
30
]
. The bibliography about exponential convergence (geometric case) for such processes is too vast and, since our main purpose in the present work is rather the study of kinetic equation like (
1.1
), we refer the reader to
[
14
]
for a nice introduction to the field. We only mention here that such geometric convergence results usually resort to hypocoercivity results and Harris-type results (see for instance
[
13
]
for an application of Harris-type techniques to the study of fragmentation equation) or to a careful spectral analysis of the associated semigroup (see
[
37
,
38
]
for very recent application to fragmentation models). For subgeometric convergence to equilibrium, a somehow concurrent approach (well-adapted to nonlinear models) is the so-called entropy method which consists in quantifying the entropy dissipation properties of the collisional operato to deduce establish the algebraic rate of convergence of the entropy (this, in turn, provides a decay in the usual
L^{1}
-norm thanks to Csiszar-Kullback inequality). For linear model, this method has been applied in
[
12
]
for the linear Boltzmann equation or its relaxation model caricature
[
9
]
.

For subgeometric convergence to equilibrium of Markov processes, Harris-type tools have been develop in the probabilistic community (see for instance
[
20
,
6
]
) and we refer again the reader to
[
14
]
for a thorough description of such results. Subgeometric convergence rates have also been studied for classical models as the Fokker-Planck
equation
[
23
]
and, for spatially homogeneous linear Boltzmann equation in a previous contribution by the authors
[
26
]
.

As far as spatially inhomogeneous kinetic equations are concerned, the question of estimating the speed of approach to equilibrium for a non-homogeneous, linear transport equation with degenerated total scattering cross-section has been considered mainly in the context of the linearized Boltzmann or Landau equation (see for instance
[
10
,
15
]
to mention just a few relevant results). Spectral gap estimates via the so-called hypocoercivity method have been derived in a
L^{2}
-setting in
[
19
,
21
]
while algebraic rate of convergence towards the equilibrium, still in the
L^{2}
setting, has been established in
[
18
]
norm is established for a case in which the cross-section
\sigma
is depending on
x
and vanishes in some portion of the space. We also mention the recent contribution
[
7
]
in which a decay similar to the one in Theorem
1.7
is obtained in a
L^{2}
framework.

For a purely
L^{1}
–approach, the literature on the field is scarcer. We mention the contribution
[
34
]
and
[
11
]
which prove the existence of a spectral gap if
\sigma
is bounded for below by means of spectral analysis and Harris-type results respectively. Harris-type of results are actually providing subgeometric rate of convergence for linear Boltzmann equation with weak confining potentials in
[
11
]
.

For “subgeometric” convergence to equilibrium for the degenerate linear kinetic equations (
1.1
) on the torus, as far as we know, the only previous work providing results similar to those obtained in the present paper is
[
24
]
in which
(V;\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v))
is a probability space (we change slightly the quick presentation of this work in order to compare it to ours). The main assumption in
[
24
]
is then

(1.22)

\begin{cases}\bm{k}(\cdot,\cdot)\in L^{\infty}(V\times V,\bm{m}\otimes\bm{m})\,,\\
\qquad\displaystyle\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime})=\displaystyle\int_{V}\bm{k}(v^{\prime},v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime}),\quad\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})\leqslant C\,\sigma(v)\sigma(v^{\prime})\\
\displaystyle\int_{V}\sigma^{-a}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)<\infty\end{cases}

for some (maximal)
a>0
. The analysis of
[
24
]
is carried out in the space

\bm{W}_{a}:=\left\{f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\;;\;\|f\|_{\bm{W}_{a}}:=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left\|\widehat{f}(p)\right\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{a})}<\infty\right\}

where, for any Fourier mode
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,
\widehat{f}(p)
is the Fourier transform of
f
in the
x
-variable

\widehat{f}(p,\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}f(x,\cdot)\exp\left(i\,x\cdot p\right)\mathrm{d}x,\qquad p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

and

\|\widehat{f}(p)\|_{L^{1}(\mu_{a})}=\int_{V}\left|\widehat{f}(p,v)\right|\sigma^{-a}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\,,\,\quad p\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

Under assumption (
1.22
), the main result in
[
24
]
is a convergence rate towards equilibrium of the type

(1.23)

\|\mathcal{V}(t)f-\varrho_{f}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{c}{(1+t)^{a}}\|f\|_{\bm{W}_{a}},\qquad t\geqslant 0.

Notice that
\|f\|_{\bm{W}_{a}}
is a combination of the Wiener algebra norm in space variable
x
and the weighted norm with weight
\sigma^{-a}
in velocity. Moreover, under assumption (
1.22
), the unique steady equilibrium state is
\Psi=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}
. The strategy of
[
24
]
is completely different from ours and is based upon some Fourier diagonalization of the transport operator
v\cdot\nabla_{x}
, the inverse Laplace transform for each Fourier mode and the use of
the theory of Fredholm determinants to derive (
1.23
). However, the result obtained is comparable to ours. Note that the boundedness of
\bm{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
and the finiteness of the measure
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
implies that
K\>:\>L^{1}(V)\to L^{1}(V)
is
weakly compact
whereas, under assumption (
1.22
), one can check without difficulty that

\vartheta_{a+1}(v)=\frac{1}{\sigma(v)}\int_{V}\sigma^{-a-1}(v^{\prime})\bm{k}(v,v^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime})\leqslant C\int_{V}\sigma^{-a}(v^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v^{\prime})<\infty

i.e. (
1.10
) holds true with
N_{0}=\lfloor a\rfloor+1
. Our main result gives then a decay rate like
(1+t)^{-N_{0}+1}=(1+t)^{-\lfloor a\rfloor}
. Note that the second assumption in (
1.22
) implies (
1.11
) which means that (
1.22
) implies all our Assumptions
1.1
. However, the construction in
[
24
]
is independent of our set of assumptions
1.5
. Even though the class of measure
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is much general in
[
24
]
than in our presentation, our main result Theorem
1.7
provides a sharper rate of convergence rate if
a\in\mathbb{N}
: using
\mathrm{O-o}
Landau’s notation, our result improves the
\mathrm{O}\left((1+t)^{-a}\right)=\mathrm{O}\left(1+t)^{-N_{0}+1}\right)
rate in (
1.23
) into a

\mathrm{o}\left((1+t)^{-N_{0}+1}\right)

rate. Moreover, our result applies to a broader class of functions since
\bm{W}_{a}
is a proper subspace of
\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}-1}=\mathbb{X}_{a}
. We also point out that the diagonilisation Fourier procedure makes the approach in
[
24
]
difficult to adapt to the case in which
\bm{k}
(and thus
\sigma
) depends on
x
whereas our approach appears robust enough to allow to tackle this case.

We finally mention, on a different but related topic, the recent works
[
4
,
3
]
and our contribution
[
27
]

consider linear transport equation for collisionless gas in which the scattering occurs only on the boundary of a bounded region and the return to equilibrium is induced by the boundary conditions. More precisely, in
[
4
,
3
]
, some
ad hoc
Harris-type results are tailored to treat such collisionless model whereas, in our contribution
[
27
]
, a tauberian approach similar to the one we consider in the present work is devised. The results in
[
27
]

served as an inspiration for the techniques developed in the present paper.

1.4.
Strategy

The general strategy to prove Theorem
1.7
is explained in full details in Section
2
in which the main steps are described. In a nutshell, we just mention here that our approach is Tauberian in essence since we will deduce the decay of the semigroup
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
for some fine properties of its Laplace transform along the imaginary axis
\lambda=i\eta
. This approach combines in a robust and efficient way the so-called
semigroup and resolvent approaches
for the study of the long-time behaviour of transport equation (see
[
32
]
for a comprehensive description of the semigroup approach and
[
31
]
for a first account of the resolvent one). As said, inspired by our results in
[
27
]
, we device here a method which combines the two approaches. In particular, using that the semigroup
\mathcal{V}(t)
is given by a Dyson-Phillips series

(1.24)

\mathcal{V}(t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}U_{n}(t)

where

(1.25)

U_{n+1}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{n}(t-s)\mathcal{K}\,U_{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geqslant 0,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}\,,

and
we first establish, for
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
a
universal
decay of each of the iterated
U_{n}(t)f
as
t\to\infty.
Second, we carefully study the behaviour of some reminder of the above Dyson-Phillips expansion

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}U_{k}(t)

and shows that its Laplace transform

\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)f=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0

can be extended, for suitable class of functions
f
, up to the imaginary axis
\lambda=i\eta
with moreover a nice decay of the Laplace transform as
|\eta|\to\infty.
The existence of such a trace of
\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)f
is based upon some important collective compactness arguments, as introduced in
[
1
]
, and the consequences of those compactness argument to the spectral theory of some of the operators defining
\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)
(see Section
3
for details). Using then the inverse Laplace transform, this allows to deduce our main decay estimate in Theorem
1.7
. As said already, this very rough description is expanded in the next Section
2
which provides for a detailed description of the main technical difficulty as well as the organization of the paper (see Section
2.4
).

We point out here that, even though our approach is inspired by our previous contribution
[
27
]
, it differs from it in several technical and conceptual aspects. In particular, a crucial role in our analysis will be played by the family of operators

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}),\qquad\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0

where
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
is the resolvent of
\mathcal{A}
which can be easily extended in a family of stochastic operators along the imaginary axis
\mathrm{Re}\lambda=0
(see Section
4
for details). One can easily get convinced here by direct computations that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+\eta}f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

but

\sup_{\varepsilon>0}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}>0.

Therefore, the mapping
\varepsilon\geqslant 0\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
is strongly continuous but is not continuous in the operator norm topology whereas, in our construction in
[
27
]
, the analogue of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
was played by a family of boundary operators was continuous in the operator norm up to
\varepsilon=0.
To be able to deduce spectral properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
from the sole strong continuity, we have to resort to several
collective compactness results
proving in particular that
there exists
\mathsf{q}>0
such that

\left\{\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact. The fact that we are dealing only with some
iterate
of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
and not with
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
itself prevents us to use
directly
known functional analysis results linking the collective compactness and the strong convergence and forces us to tailor some specific extension of the results of
[
1
]
here. This use of collective compactness is one of main difference between the the approach followed in
[
27
]
and the one cooked up for the present contribution.

2.
General strategy and main results

Our general strategy to investigate the time-decay of
\mathcal{V}(t)f
is based upon a general and robust approach which fully exploits semigroup and resolvent interplays.

2.1.
Preliminary facts

We recall that
(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K},\mathscr{D}(\mathcal{A}))
generates a
C_{0}
-semigroup
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}

given by (
1.24
) and (
1.25
)
and the key basic observation is the decay of
U_{0}(t)
on the hierarchy of spaces
\mathbb{X}_{k}
, namely

Lemma 2.1
.

Given
k\geqslant 0
, one has

(2.1)

\left\|U_{0}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\left(\frac{k}{e\,t}\right)^{k}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}},\qquad\forall t>0,\qquad f\in\mathbb{X}_{k}.

Moreover, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
, it holds

(2.2)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left\|U_{0}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k+1}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left\|U_{0}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\Gamma(k+1)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k+1}}

where
\Gamma(\cdot)
is the usual Gamma function.

Proof of Lemma
2.1
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k}
and
t>0
be fixed. For simplicity, we introduce
g(x,v)=\sigma(v)^{-k}|f(x,v)|
,
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
. One has then

(2.3)

\begin{split}\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(v)^{k}e^{-t\sigma(v)}|g(x-tv,v)|\mathrm{d}x\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(v)^{k}e^{-t\sigma(v)}|g(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\end{split}

where we performed the change of variable
y=x-tv
,
\mathrm{d}y=\mathrm{d}x.
Using the elementary inequality

(2.4)

u^{k}e^{-u}\leqslant\left(\frac{k}{e}\right)^{k}\qquad\forall u\geqslant 0

and applying it with
u=t\sigma(v)
, one has

\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\left(\frac{k}{t\,e}\right)^{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|g(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\left(\frac{k}{t\,e}\right)^{k}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(v)^{-k}|f(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)

which gives (
2.1
). Now, one deduces also from (
2.3
) and Fubini’s theorem that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\|U_{0}(t)g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}e^{-t\sigma(v)}|g(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|g(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}t=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(v)^{-1}(v)|g(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k+1}}

whereas

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}t&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}e^{-t\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma^{-k}(v)|f(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(t\sigma(v)\right)^{k}e^{-t\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma^{-k-1}(v)|f(y,v)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{k}e^{-\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau\end{split}

where we performed the change of variable
\tau=t\,\sigma(v)
in the last step. This gives the last estimate.
∎

2.2.
Decay of the Dyson-Phillips iterated

We extend the decay of the semigroup
\left(U_{0}(t))\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
obtained in Lemma
2.1
to the iterates

\left(U_{k}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
for any
k\geqslant 1
. To do so, we first observe that for any
t\geqslant 0
,
U_{0}(t)
commute with any multiplication operator depending on the velocity i.e.

U_{0}(t)(\varpi\,f)=\varpi\,U_{0}(t)f

for any
\varpi=\varpi(v)\in L^{\infty}(V)
. Moreover,
U_{0}(t)
has an exponential decay on any region in which
\sigma
is bounded away from zero. Namely, for any
\delta>0
, introduce

\Lambda_{\delta}:=\{v\in V\;;\;\sigma(v)\geqslant\delta\},\qquad\Sigma_{\delta}=V\setminus\Lambda_{\delta}

one has

\|U_{0}(t)\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant e^{-t\delta}\|\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant e^{-t\delta}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\qquad t\geqslant 0.

Introducing for any
\delta>0
, the operator
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
given by

(2.5)

\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}f(x,v)=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\mathcal{K}f(x,v)\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\quad(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V

as well as

(2.6)

\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}=\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\,,

one can check the following

Lemma 2.2
.

For any
n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
,

(2.7)

\|\mathcal{K}-{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\delta^{n}\|\vartheta_{n}\|_{\infty},\qquad\qquad\forall 0\leqslant n\leqslant N_{0}.

Having such a property in mind, we can deduce the decay of
U_{k}(t)
as
t\to\infty
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
resulting in

Proposition 2.3
.

Assume that

f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}

then, for any
n\geqslant 1
, there exists
\bm{C}_{n}>0
such that

(2.8)

\left\|\sum_{k=0}^{n}U_{k}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\bm{C}_{n}\,\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{N_{0}}\left\|f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}\qquad\forall t>0.

The proof is based on a splitting of each term
U_{k}(t)
as

U_{k}(t)=U_{k}^{(\delta)}(t)+\overline{U}_{k}^{(\delta)}(t)

where
U_{k}^{(\delta)}(t)
is constructed as a Dyson-Phillips iterated involving
only
the operator
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
whereas the reminder terms make appear
at least once
the difference
\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
and, as such, can be made small with respect to
\delta
by virtue of Lemma
2.2
. For the part involving only
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
, we are dealing with a Dyson-Phillips iterate associated with a collision frequency which is
bounded away from zero
we can deduce a full exponential decay of
U_{k}^{(\delta)}(t)
and, optimizing the parameter
\delta
, we deduce the algebraic decay of
U_{k}(t)
. Details are given in Appendix
A
.

2.3.
Representation formulae for remainder terms

On the basis of Proposition
2.3
, one sees that, to capture a decay of
\mathcal{V}(t)f
, it is enough to focus on the decay of the reminders

(2.9)

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t):=\mathcal{V}(t)-\sum_{k=0}^{n}U_{k}(t),\qquad n\geqslant 0,\;\,t>0.

This is where the resolvent approach enters in the game since it will be convenient to observe that such a reminder admits a useful representation formula as an inverse Laplace transform.
We recall here that, for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0
, the resolvent of
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}
exists and is given by

(2.10)

\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}

where the series converge in operator norm.

Before such a representation, let us explain here the main important consequences of Assumptions
1.1
–
1.5
we refer to Section
3
for a complete proof.

Theorem 2.4
.

If
\mathcal{K}
satisfies Assumptions
1.1
, then there exists
\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left\{\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{\mathsf{q}}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact. Moreover, if
\mathcal{K}
satisfies also Assumptions
1.5
, there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

(2.11)

\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}+\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{C_{0}}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{0\}.

In particular, for any
\mathsf{p}>4
, there is
C_{\mathsf{p}}>0
such that

(2.12)

\sup_{\varepsilon\geqslant 0}\int_{|\eta|>1}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{\mathsf{p}}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta\leqslant C_{\mathsf{p}}<\infty.

Remark 2.5
.

We point here that the collective compactness properties is a consequence of Assumption (
1.11
) and is crucial in particular for the existence of an invariant density
\Psi
of
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
in Theorem
1.2
(we refer to
[
36
]
for details on that matter).

It is well-established that the reminder
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)
of the Dyson-Phillips series here above admits the following Laplace transform:

(2.13)

\begin{split}\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)f&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(\lambda t\right)\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{k+1}f,\qquad\forall\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0,f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.\end{split}

This allows to express in a natural way
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
as the inverse Laplace transform of
\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)

(\eta\in\mathbb{R},\varepsilon>0)
(see Proposition
A.5
in Appendix
A
).

The crucial point in our analysis is then to extend the representation formula (
A.13
)
up to the boundary
\varepsilon=0
. Of course, since
0\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A})
we cannot expect to define
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda=0
using (
2.10
). However, under assumption (
1.8
), it is possible to extend the definition of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
to
\lambda=0
by observing that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\varphi=\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi,\qquad\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

exists with, for almost every
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
,

\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\varphi(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t\,.

Notice indeed that, for
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,
\varphi\geqslant 0
,

(2.14)

\begin{split}\|\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi\|_{0}&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\varphi(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi(y,w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi(y,w)\mathrm{d}y\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi(y,w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\end{split}

where we used the change of variable
y=x-tw
in the second identity and (
1.8
) for the last one. Thus,
\mathsf{M}_{0}
is stochastic, i.e. mass-preserving on the positive cone of
\mathbb{X}_{0}
:

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=\|\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\;\varphi\geqslant 0.

In particular,

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=1.

More generally, it is possible to define, for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f,\qquad f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

The properties of the operator
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
are the cornerstone of our analysis which, somehow, culminates with the following result :

Theorem 2.6
.

Let Assumptions
1.1
and
1.5
be in force, Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
be such that

(2.15)

\varrho_{f}=\int_{\Omega\times V}f(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=0.

Then, the following holds:

(1)

For any
n\geqslant 0
the limit

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathscr{S}_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f,

exists in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. Its limit is denoted
{\Upsilon}_{n}(\eta)f
.

(2)

For any
n\geqslant 5\cdot 2^{N_{0}-1}
, the trace function

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

and its derivatives of order
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
are integrable, i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty\qquad\forall k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}.

Consequently, for
n\geqslant 5\cdot 2^{N_{0}-1}-1
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
satisfying (
2.15
),
one has

(2.16)

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta,\qquad\forall t>0

where the convergence holds in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
and

(2.17)

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f=\left(-\frac{i}{t}\right)^{N_{0}-1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{N_{0}-1}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{N_{0}-1}}\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\,\mathrm{d}\eta

holds true for any
t\geqslant 0
where the convergence of the integral holds in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
.

We admit this result for a little while, the whole rest of the paper being devoted to a complete proof of this fundamental Theorem. Let us illustrate right away how to deduce our main result from Theorem
2.6
:

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Let us fix
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
. To prove the result, we can assume without loss of generality that
\varrho_{f}=0
. Of course, the term
\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\cdot)
is given by

\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{N_{0}-1}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{N_{0}-1}}\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\qquad\eta\in\mathbb{R}

for some suitable choice of
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall first that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
t\geqslant 0

\mathcal{V}(t)f=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{U}_{k}(t)f=\sum_{k=0}^{n}{U}_{k}(t)+\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f

where, according to Proposition
2.3
,

\left\|\sum_{k=0}^{n}{U}_{k}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{n}\left(\frac{\log}{1+t}\right)^{-N_{0}},\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0

for some positive constant
C_{n}
depending on
n
and
f
(but not on
t
). Choosing now
n\geqslant 2^{N_{0}-1}\mathsf{p}
and using (
2.17
), one obtains

\left\|\mathcal{V}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{n}(1+t)^{-N_{0}-1}+t^{-N_{0}-1}\mathcal{F}_{n}(t)

where

\mathcal{F}_{n}(t)=\left\|\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{N_{0}-1}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{N_{0}-1}}\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

is such that
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{n}(t)=0
according to Riemann-Lebesgue Theorem (recall the mapping
\eta\mapsto\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{N_{0}-1}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{N_{0}-1}}\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
is integrable over
\mathbb{R}
according to (
2.17
)). This proves the first part of the result.

Let us now prove the second part of it. According to (
2.11
), one deduces very easily that, for any
\mathsf{p}>4
, there is
C_{\mathsf{p}}>0
such that

\|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{\mathsf{p}}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{\mathsf{p}}\,|\eta|^{-\frac{\mathsf{p}}{4}},\qquad\forall|\eta|>1

from which, for
R>1
,

(2.18)

\int_{|\eta|>R}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{\mathsf{p}}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta\leqslant\frac{8C_{\mathsf{p}}}{\mathsf{p}-4}R^{-\frac{\mathsf{p}-4}{4}}=:\tilde{C}(\mathsf{p})R^{-\beta},\qquad\forall R>1

with
\beta=\frac{\mathsf{p}-4}{4}.
Since the mapping

\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}\>:\>\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{N_{0}-1}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{N_{0}-1}}\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
, it is uniformly continuous. This allows to define a (minimal) modulus of continuity

\omega_{f}(s):=\sup\left\{\left\|\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta_{1})-\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta_{2})\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\,;\,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\,|\eta_{1}-\eta_{2}|\leqslant s\right\},\quad s\geqslant 0.

The estimate then comes from some standard reasoning about Fourier transform. Namely, introducing the Fourier transform of the (Bochner integrable) function
\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}
as

\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp(i\eta t)\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\qquad t\geqslant 0

one has then, since
e^{i\pi}=-1=\exp(i\pi t/t),

t>0
,

\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(i\eta t+i\frac{\pi}{t}t\right)\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\mathrm{d}\eta=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(iyt\right)\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}\left(y-\frac{\pi}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}y

which gives, taking the mean of both the expressions of
\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)
,

\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\left(\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)-\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}\left(\eta-\frac{\pi}{t}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}\eta.

Consequently, if one assumes that
R>2\pi
,

\left\|\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\int_{|\eta|\leqslant R}\left\|\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)-\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}\left(\eta-\frac{\pi}{t}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta+\int_{|\eta|>\frac{R}{2}}\left\|\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta

where we used that
\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}\;;\;|\eta+\frac{\pi}{t}|>R\}\subset\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}\;;\;|\eta|>R-\pi\}\subset\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}\;;\;|\eta|>\frac{R}{2}\}
since
t\geqslant 1,

R-\pi>\frac{R}{2}
. Therefore, using the modulus of continuity
\omega_{f}
and (
2.18
), we deduce that

(2.19)

\begin{split}\left\|\widehat{\mathsf{\Theta}}_{f}(t)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant R\omega_{f}\left(\frac{\pi}{t}\right)+\int_{|\eta|>\frac{R}{2}}\left\|\mathsf{\Theta}_{f}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta\\
&\leqslant R\omega_{f}\left(\frac{\pi}{t}\right)+2^{\beta}\tilde{C}(\mathsf{p})R^{-\beta}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}},\qquad\forall R>2\pi,\qquad t\geqslant 1.\end{split}

Optimising then the parameter
R
, i.e. choosing

R=\left(\frac{2^{\beta}\beta\,\tilde{C}(\mathsf{p})\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}}{\omega_{f}\left(\frac{\pi}{t}\right)}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta+1}}

(up to work with
t\geqslant t_{0}\geqslant 1
to ensure that
R>2\pi
), we obtain the desired estimate since
\frac{\beta}{\beta+1}=\frac{\mathsf{p-4}}{\mathsf{p}}
.∎

2.4.
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.6
. More precisely, in Section
3
we deduce from Assumptions
1.1
the main collective compactness properties and decay estimates which will play a fundamental role for the proof of Theorem
2.6
. In Section
4
, we established the preliminary results about the regularity of some of the terms appearing in the resolvent
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
and establish the existence of some of their limit along the imaginary axis
\lambda=i\eta
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. In Section
5
, we especially focus on the spectral properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
and, in a particular way, on its spectral behaviour around
\lambda=0
. We use, in a crucial way in this part, Anselone’s collective compactness theory and the results established in Section
3
. Section
6
establish the existence of the extension of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
to the imaginary axis. In Section
7
, we finally provide the full proof of Theorem
2.6
. The paper ends with three Appendices containing several of the main technical aspects of the proofs. Namely, Appendix
A
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.3
, Appendix
B
establishes some of the technical properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
used in Section
5
and Appendix
C

recalls the main aspects of Anselone’s collective compactness theory we use in the paper.

3.
Consequences of Assumptions
1.1
and
1.5

We comment here on the main consequences of our set of Assumptions on the operator
\mathcal{K}
given in Assumptions
1.1
. Namely, the following two Theorems
3.1
and
3.5
provides a complete proof of Theorem
2.4
.

3.1.
A criterion for collective compactness of some power

We begin with by showing how Assumption
1.1
is the key argument to deduce the collective compactness of some power of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
in Theorem
2.4
.
Our scope is to prove the following

Theorem 3.1
.

Let us assume that
\mathcal{K}\>:\>L^{1}(V)\to L^{1}(V)
is a
weakly compact
operator and its kernel
satisfies (
1.8
) together with (
1.11
). We also assume the measure
\bm{m}
to satisfy (
1.12
).
Then, there exists
\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{N}
such that the family of operators

\left\{\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact.

Remark 3.2
.

We insist here on the fact that the collective compactness provided by Theorem
3.1
is the crucial argument in the proof of an invariant density
\Psi
in Theorem
1.2
as obtained in
[
36
]
.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
resorts on similar results in
[
36
]
and on a suitable approximation argument. We use here notations of Section
2.2
. Namely, for any
\delta>0
, we recall from (
2.5
)

\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\>:\>\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\mapsto\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\varphi(x,v)=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}(v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\varphi(x,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

It is clear that
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
with
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\varphi(x,v)\leqslant\mathcal{K}\varphi(x,v)
for any
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,
\varphi\geqslant 0.

One has the following:

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\sup_{\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0}\left\|\bigg[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\bigg]^{n}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant n\mu_{\delta}

where

\mu_{\delta}=\sup_{w\in V}\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{\Sigma_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v),\qquad\quad\Sigma_{\delta}=V\setminus\Lambda_{\delta}=\{v\in V\;;\;\sigma(v)\leqslant\delta\}.

Proof.

The proof is made by induction over
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Observing that
\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
is an integral operator of the form

(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)})\varphi(x,v)=\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\delta}}(v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\varphi(x,w)\mathrm{d}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

one already saw that

\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\|(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)})\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

Since

\|\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\sup_{w\in V}\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{V}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\delta}}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\mu_{\delta}

this proves the result for
n=1
. Let us assume the result to be true for some
n\geqslant 1.
One has

\bigg[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\bigg]^{n+1}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n+1}=\left(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\\
+\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left(\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\right).

Observing that

\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 1,

for any
\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0,

n\in\mathbb{N}
, one deduces that

\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n+1}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n+1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\left\|\left(\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
+\left\|\bigg[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\bigg]^{n}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

Using the induction hypothesis, one sees that

\left\|\bigg[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\bigg]^{n+1}-\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n+1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\mu_{\delta}+n\mu_{\delta}=(n+1)\mu_{\delta}

which proves the result.
∎

We now recall some result which is somehow proven in
[
36
, Theorem 18]
(but not stated as below):

Lemma 3.4
.

Let us assume that
\mathcal{K}\>:\>L^{1}(V)\to L^{1}(V)
is a
weakly compact
operator and its kernel
satisfies (
1.8
) together with (
1.11
) while the measure
\bm{m}
satisfies (
1.12
). There exists
N
large enough (depending only on
\bm{m}
) such that, for any
\delta>0
,

\left\{\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{N}\;;\;\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact

Proof.

Recall the definition of the Dyson-Phillips (
1.24
)–(
1.25
). Since the measure
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\bm{m}(v)\mathrm{d}v
satisfies (
1.12
), one can apply
[
34
, Theorem 13]
to assert that there exists
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
U_{j}(t)
is compact for any
j\geqslant N_{0}
and any
t\geqslant 0
.
Let us now consider
\delta>0
and recall that
\Lambda_{\delta}=\{v\in V\;;\sigma(v)\geqslant\delta\}
and
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\mathcal{K}
. One observes that

\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})

where
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}=\mathcal{A}\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}
is identified with the advection operator
\mathcal{A}
on
\Lambda_{\delta}
. We also define the sequence of Dyson-Phillips iterated
\left(U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\right)_{j}
associated to
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}=\mathcal{A}\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}
and
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
, i.e.

U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{0}^{(\delta)}(t-s)\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}U_{j-1}^{(\delta)}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad j\geqslant 1

and
\left(U_{0}^{(\delta)}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
is the
C_{0}
-semigroup in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
generated by
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}=\mathcal{A}\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}
. One observes that

U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\leqslant U_{j}(t),\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0,\quad j\in\mathbb{N}

Therefore, by a domination argument,
U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)
is weakly-compact for any
j\geqslant N_{0}
and, consequently,
U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)
is compact for any
j\geqslant N_{1}=2N_{0}+1
(see
[
32
, Theorem 2.6 and Corollary 2.1, p. 16]
). It follows then that the mapping

t\geqslant 0\mapsto U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is continuous (in operator norm) for
j\geqslant N_{2}=N_{1}+1
(see
[
32
, Corollary 2.2, p. 10]
).

Now, observe that, on
\Lambda_{\delta}
the collision frequency is bounded from below, one has

s(\mathcal{A}_{\delta})<0.

Then, since

\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{j}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})

where the integral is converging in operator norm for any
\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0
, we deduce that

\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{N_{2}+1}\quad\text{ is compact for any }\mathrm{Re}\lambda>s(\mathcal{A}_{\delta}).

Since, for any
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>s(\mathcal{A}_{\delta})
, the mapping

t\geqslant 0\mapsto\exp\left(-i\,t\,\mathrm{Im}\lambda\right)\left[\exp\left(-t\,\mathrm{Re}\lambda\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\right]\mathrm{d}t\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is
Bochner integrable
, one has

\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{j}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})=\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\mathrm{d}t

is compact for
j\geqslant N_{2}
and
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>s(\mathcal{A}_{\delta})
and, by Riemann-Lebesgue Theorem,

\lim_{|\mathrm{Im}\lambda|\to\infty}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(-i\,t\,\mathrm{Im}\lambda\right)\left[\exp\left(-t\mathrm{Re}\lambda\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=0

according to Riemann-Lebesgue Theorem. It follows that

\lim_{|\mathrm{Im}\lambda|\to\infty}\sup_{\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]}\left\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp\left(-i\,t\,\mathrm{Im}\lambda\right)\left[\exp\left(-t\mathrm{Re}\lambda\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(t)\right]\mathrm{d}t\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=0.

Indeed,

\left\{\exp\left(-\bullet\mathrm{Re}\lambda\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(\bullet)\;;\;\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]\right\}

is a compact subset of
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\,,\,\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0}))
since the mapping

\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]\mapsto\exp\left(-\bullet\mathrm{Re}\lambda\right)U_{j}^{(\delta)}(\bullet)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+},\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0}))

is continuous. Thus,

\lim_{|\mathrm{Im}\lambda|\to\infty}\sup_{\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{N_{2}+1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=0,\qquad\forall\delta>0

and finally

\left\{\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{N_{2}+1},\quad 0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}

is collectively compact. One gets the conclusion since
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

The previous Lemma
3.3
shows that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}
can be approximated in the operator norm by
\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}
as
\delta\to 0

uniformly with respect to

\lambda
in the set

\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]\}.

Therefore, to prove Theorem
3.1
, it is enough to prove that there exists
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for
any fixed

\delta\in[0,1]
, the set of operators

\left\{\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{m}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact. One notices now that, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geqslant 2
,

(3.1)

\left[\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{m}=\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{m-2}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}),\qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

We deduce from the previous Lemma that we can find
m
large enough (
independent of
\delta
) so that

\left\{\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{m-2}\;;\;\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact.
Since

\left|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\varphi\right|\leqslant\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathsf{M}_{0}|\varphi|

for any
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
with
\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})
and
\mathsf{M}_{0}
both bounded. One deduces that

\left\{\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\varphi\;;\;\|\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant 1\;\;;\;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}

is a bounded subset of
\mathbb{X}_{0}.
From the collective compactness of
\left\{\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}_{\delta})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{m-2}\right\}_{\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]}
, one deduces that

\left\{\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\right]^{m-2}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\varphi\;\;;\;\|\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant 1\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}

is included in some compact of
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. This ends the proof since
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
.
∎

3.2.
Decay of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}

The scope here is to prove the following result

Theorem 3.5
.

If
\mathcal{K}
satisfies Assumption
1.5
, there exists
C>0
such that

(3.2)

\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}+\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{C}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}},\qquad\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\quad|\lambda|\geqslant 1.

We will divide the proof in several lemmas which contain the crucial estimates of our analysis. We begin with the following easy consequences of Assumption
1.5

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{K}
satisfy Assumption
1.5
and introduce

\bm{K}_{2}(v,w^{\prime},w)=\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(w),\qquad\forall v,w,w^{\prime}\in V^{3}.

Then, there exists
C>0
such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\right|\max\left(1,\sigma^{-1}(v)\right)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
\leqslant C\,\sigma(w^{\prime})\,,\qquad\forall w^{\prime}\in V,

Moreover,

\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\sigma(w)\right|\leqslant C_{1}\sigma(w),\qquad\forall w\in V.

Proof.

Noticing that

w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)=\left[\bm{k}(v,w)\left(w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\right)+\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\left(w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(v,w)\right)\right]\bm{m}(w)\\
+\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{m}(w)\,,

it holds, for
s=0,1
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\right|\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-1}(w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(v,w)\right|\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-1}(w)\left(\sigma(w)\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\right|\right)\vartheta_{s}(w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\vartheta_{s}(w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\log\bm{m}(w)\right|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

which results in

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\right|\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\leqslant C\sigma(w^{\prime})

with
C=C_{1}+\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\left(C_{2}+\sup_{w}|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\log\bm{m}(w)|\right)<\infty
where we used (
1.7
), (
1.14
)–(
1.13
) and the conservative assumption (
1.8
). For the estimate of
w\cdot\nabla\sigma(w)
, one simply observes that, due to the conservative assumption

w\cdot\nabla\sigma(w)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)

so that

\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\sigma(w)\right|\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{k}(v,w)|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\leqslant C_{1}\sigma(w)

according to (
1.14
).
∎

Let
\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta
,
\varepsilon\geqslant 0,

\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. The proof of Theorem
3.5
consists in actually estimating

\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

One checks easily that

\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\,f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\mathrm{d}t\int_{V}\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})f(x-tw,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime}).

Therefore

\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\,f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})f(x-tw,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\,.

We split the integral over time into
\int_{\delta}^{\infty}+\int_{0}^{\delta}
and write accordingly

\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)+\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)

where, in
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)
, we restrict the time integral to the set
[\delta,\infty)
and
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)
is defined with the time integral over
(0,\delta)
. The estimate of
\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}
is the easiest one.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
\delta>0
it holds

\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\delta\,\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}

for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
and any
s\leqslant N_{0}
(where we recall that
\vartheta_{0}\equiv 1
).

Proof.

By definition

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\int_{0}^{\delta}\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})f(x-tw,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1},

(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V,

\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Thus,

\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{V}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}t\int_{V}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})|f(x-tw,w^{\prime})|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime}).

One easily sees, with the change of variables
x\mapsto y=x-tw
that

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}&\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,w^{\prime})|\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\\
&\phantom{++++++}\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathrm{d}t\int_{V\times V}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&\leqslant\delta\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(w^{\prime})|f(y,w^{\prime})|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\end{split}

where we used that
\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0,

\sigma\geqslant 0
and

\int_{V\times V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})=\int_{V}\sigma(w)\vartheta_{s}(w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\,.

This gives the desired estimate.
∎

It remains to estimate
\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}.
One begins with the following representation of
\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
,
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and any
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
one has

(3.3)

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{[0,1]^{d}\times V}\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})f(y,w^{\prime})\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})

where

\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(z,v,w^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\,\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right)\times\\
\times\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{z-k}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},v,w\in V.

Proof.

By definition,

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\int_{\delta}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})f(x-tw,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w).

We recall that functions over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
are identified with functions defined over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which are
[0,1]^{d}
-periodic. Given
t\geqslant\delta
, one performs the change of variable

(3.4)

y:=x-tw,\qquad\mathrm{d}y=t^{d}\mathrm{d}w,

and

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times V}f(y,w^{\prime})\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\\
\mathbf{1}_{\frac{x-y}{t}\in V}(t)\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\right)\,\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{x-y}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t\,.

Given
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d},w^{\prime}\in V
, we introduce the function

H_{\lambda}(z,v,w^{\prime})=\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\mathbf{1}_{\frac{z}{t}\in V}(t)\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{z}{t}\right)\right)\,\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{z}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{z}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{z}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t

so that

\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times V}H_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})f(y,w^{\prime})\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime}).

Notice that
\mathbb{R}^{d}
admits the following partition representation

\mathbb{R}^{d}=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(k+[0,1]^{d}\right)

so that, using the
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodicity of
f(\cdot,w^{\prime})
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)f(x,v)&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{k+[0,1]^{d}}\mathrm{d}y\int_{V}H_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})f(y,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\\
&=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{[0,1]^{d}\times V}H_{\lambda}(x-y-k,v,w^{\prime})f(y,w^{\prime})\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\end{split}

which results in (
3.3
).
∎

With this representation, the key estimate is provided by the following

Lemma 3.9
.

There exists
C_{3}>0
(depending only on
d,C_{1},C_{2},\|\sigma\|_{\infty}
,
\|v\cdot\nabla_{v}\log\bm{m}\|_{\infty}
and
\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}
) such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\max\left(1,\sigma^{-1}(v)\right)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\leqslant\frac{{C}_{3}}{|\lambda|}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+1\right)\sigma(w^{\prime})

for any
y,w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
,
\delta>0,

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\{0\}.

Proof.

We begin with the following observation, valid for any
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
and which is obtained thanks to a simple use of integration by parts,

\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left[t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right]\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
-\frac{1}{\lambda}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\bigg|_{t=\delta}^{\infty}\\
=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left[t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right]\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
+\frac{1}{\lambda}\delta^{-d}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(\frac{u}{\delta}\right)\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{\delta}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{\delta},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{\delta}\right)\exp\left(-\lambda\delta\right)

Recalling that we introduced
\bm{K}_{2}(v,w^{\prime},w)=\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(w),
for any
v,w,w^{\prime}\in V^{3},
one sees that, for
v,u,w^{\prime}\in V^{3}
and
t>\delta
, one has
alors

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left[t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right]\\
=t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\,\left[-\frac{d}{t}-\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)+\frac{u}{t}\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right]\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right)\\
+t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\,\left(-\frac{u}{t^{2}}\cdot\nabla_{3}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)

where
\nabla_{3}\bm{K}_{2}(v,w^{\prime},w)
denotes the gradient with respect to the third variable
w\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.
In particular

\frac{1}{t}\nabla_{3}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right)=\nabla_{u}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right).

From this, one has

\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\bm{k}\left(v,\frac{u}{t}\right)\bm{k}\left(\frac{u}{t},w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\,\left[-\frac{d}{t}-\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)+\frac{u}{t}\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right]\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right)\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
-\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{u}{t}\right)\right)\frac{u}{t}\cdot\nabla_{u}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{t}\right)\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
+\frac{1}{\lambda}\delta^{-d}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(\frac{u}{\delta}\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{u}{\delta}\right)\exp\left(-\lambda\delta\right)\,.

Consequently, recalling that

\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(z,v,w^{\prime})=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t

one deduces that

\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(z,v,w^{\prime})\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\\
\left|-\frac{d}{t}-\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)+\frac{z-k}{t}\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}t\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right)\left|\frac{z-k}{t}\cdot\nabla_{z}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{z-k}{t}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}t\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\delta^{-d}\sum_{k\in Z^{d}}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(\frac{z-k}{\delta}\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{z-k}{\delta}\right).

Therefore, using periodicity once again,

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\right)\\
\left(\frac{d}{\delta}+\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)+\left|\frac{x-y}{t}\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\right|\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\mathrm{d}x\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\delta}^{\infty}t^{-d}\mathrm{d}t\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-t\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\right)\left|\frac{x-y}{t}\cdot\nabla_{x}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{x-y}{t}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}x\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\delta^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(\frac{x-y}{\delta}\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},\frac{x-y}{\delta}\right)\mathrm{d}x

Performing the backward change of variables with respect to (
3.4
), i.e. setting
x\mapsto w=\frac{x-y}{t}
for the first two integrals and using that

\int_{\delta}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\frac{1}{\sigma(w)},\qquad\forall w\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

one deduces that

\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{d}{\delta}+\sigma\left(w\right)+\left|w\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(w\right)\right|\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\right|\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(w\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\mathrm{d}w

where we performed the change of variable
x\mapsto w=\frac{x-y}{\delta}
for the last integral. Integrating now with respect to
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we deduce that, for
s=0,1
,
