\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\\
\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left(\frac{d}{\delta}+\sigma\left(w\right)+\left|w\cdot\nabla\sigma\left(w\right)\right|\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|w\cdot\nabla_{w}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\right|\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}w\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
+\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\left(-\delta\sigma\left(w\right)\right)\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\mathrm{d}w\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\,.

Noticing that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{K}_{2}\left(v,w^{\prime},w\right)\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\sigma(w)\vartheta_{s}(w)\bm{k}(w,w^{\prime})\bm{m}(w)

and using Lemma
3.6
, we obtain that, or
s=0,1

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\\
\leqslant\frac{1}{|\lambda|}\left(\frac{d}{\delta}+\|\sigma\|_{\infty}+C_{1}\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\right)\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{k}\left(w,w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
+\frac{C}{|\lambda|}\sigma(w^{\prime})+\frac{\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}}{|\lambda|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\bm{k}\left(w,w^{\prime}\right)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\,

from which we easily derive the conclusion.
∎

Remark 3.10
.

We insist on the fact that the change of variables (
3.4
) (and its backward counterpart) is the only part of our analysis in which we use the fact that
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure. We strongly believe that, at a price of additional technical calculations, our analysis can be extended to a larger class of measures
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
satisfying (
1.12
).

We have all at hands to prove the result

Proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Recalling that, for any
\delta>0
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}

\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{U}_{\delta}(\lambda)+\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)

we deduce from (
3.3
) that, for
s=0,1
,

\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{U}_{\delta}^{\prime}(\lambda)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\\
+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w^{\prime})\int_{V}\sigma^{-s}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{[0,1]^{d}}\left|\widetilde{H}_{\lambda}(x-y,v,w^{\prime})\right|\mathrm{d}x\\
\leqslant\delta\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}+\frac{C_{3}}{|\lambda|}\left(\frac{1}{\delta}+1\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}

where we used Lemmas
3.7
and
3.9
in the second estimate. Optimizing now the parameter
\delta
, we deduce that

\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{s})}\leqslant 2\sqrt{\frac{C_{3}\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}{|\lambda|}}+\frac{C_{3}}{|\lambda|},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\setminus\{0\}.

For
s=0
, we deduce from this that there exists
C_{4}>0
such that, for
|\lambda|>1
,

\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{-1})}\leqslant\frac{C_{4}}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}}

since
\sup_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{-1})}\leqslant 1.
Now, with
s=1
, we deduce in the same way that there is
C_{5}>0
such that

\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\leqslant\frac{C_{5}}{\sqrt{|\lambda|}}

since
\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{1})}
.
This proves the result. ∎

4.
Regularity and extension results

From now, we will always assume that Assumptions
1.1
and
1.5
are in force.

4.1.
About the resolvent of
\mathcal{A}

We recall that under Assumption (
1.9
) one has

0\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A}).

More precisely, since
\mathcal{A}
generates a
C_{0}
-group in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
, one can prove (see
[
33
]
) that there exists
\lambda_{\star}>0
such that

(4.1)

\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A})=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;-\lambda_{\star}\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 0\}.

This shows in particular that

i\eta\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A})\qquad\qquad\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

Regarding the behaviour of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f
, one first observes that, by virtue of (
4.1
)

(4.2)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\infty.

However, studying the behaviour of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f
on the hierarchy of spaces
\mathbb{X}_{k}
,
k\geqslant 1
yields better estimates:

Proposition 4.1
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
and
\varepsilon>0
, the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}.
Moreover, given
s\in\mathbb{R}
, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s+1}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f

exists in
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{s})
. This limit is denoted
\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f,
i.e.

(4.3)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{s+1}.

Proof.

We begin with the first part of the Proposition.
Observing that

\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}e^{-\varepsilon t}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t

where the mapping
t\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto e^{-\varepsilon t}U_{0}(t)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
is Bochner integrable, one deduces from Riemann-Lebesgue Theorem that the mapping
\eta\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
, in particular

(4.4)

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, because

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=(-i)^{k}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}e^{-i\eta t}e^{-\varepsilon t}U_{0}(t)f\,\mathrm{d}t

the exact same argument shows that

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

which proves that
\eta\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f
belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0}).
Let us focus now on the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.
Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
be given, we deduce from Lemma
2.1
and the dominated convergence theorem that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}e^{-\varepsilon t}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. The limit is of course denoted
\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f
and one has

\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|e^{-\varepsilon t}-1\right|\,\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\,\qquad\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R},\,\varepsilon>0.

Thus

(4.5)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

still using the fact that
t\mapsto\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}
is integrable over
[0,\infty)
and the dominated convergence theorem. Now, given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s+1}
, setting

g(x,v)=\sigma^{s}(v)f(x,v)

one sees that
g\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
and
U_{0}(f)g(x,v)=\sigma^{s}(v)U_{0}(t)f(x,v)
. Applying (
4.5
) to
g
, we deduce that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0

and the result follows.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

One deduces from the above Proposition and Banach-Steinhaus Theorem
[
8
, Theorem 2.2, p. 32]
that

(4.6)

C_{s}:=\sup\left\{\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{s+1},\mathbb{X}_{s})}\;;\;\varepsilon\in(0,1]\;;\;\eta\in\mathbb{R}\right\}<\infty\qquad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

We deduce then the following

Corollary 4.3
.

Given
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a given compact interval. If

g\>:\>\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\longmapsto g(\lambda)\in\mathbb{X}_{s+1}

is a continuous mapping such that the limit

\widetilde{g}(\eta):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}g(\varepsilon+i\eta)

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{s+1}
uniformly with respect to
\eta\in I
. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})g(\varepsilon+i\eta)=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\widetilde{g}(\eta)

in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
where the convergence is uniform with respect to
\eta\in I
.

Proof.

The proof is a simple adaptation of the one in
[
27
, Corollary 4.14]
. Details are omitted.∎

Remark 4.4
.

If
I=\mathbb{R}
, the above result still holds true under the additional assumption that

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\|g(\varepsilon+i\eta)\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}}=0\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0

and, in such a case, the convergence of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})g(\varepsilon+i\eta)
towards
\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\widetilde{g}(\eta)
holds in
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{s})
.

The convergence established in Prop.
4.1
extends to derivatives of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f

Lemma 4.5
.

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
, it holds

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Consequently, the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0}).

Proof.

As already established

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=(-i)^{k}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}t^{k}e^{-\varepsilon t}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\varepsilon>0

and, since

\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t

one sees easily that, if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=(-i)^{k}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\eta t}t^{k}U_{0}(t)f\mathrm{d}t

is well-defined in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
thanks to Lemma
2.1
. One concludes then exactly as in Prop.
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The above formula extends trivially to derivative of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
with respect to
\lambda
and one has

(4.7)

\sup_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,A)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left\|U_{0}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\Gamma(k+1)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k+1}}

thanks to (
2.2
).

4.2.
Definition and properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}

The above results allow us to define a
bounded
linear operator

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

as the
strong limit
of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, i.e.

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\varphi:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\varphi,\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

where the limit is meant in
\mathbb{X}_{0}.
Indeed, recall that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}=0

and, since
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})
, one deduces that

(4.8)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0,

where

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0}).

Remark 4.7
.

It is easy to check that
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
given by,

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f(x,v):=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\left(i\eta+\sigma(w)\right)\right)f(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

In particular, one sees that

\begin{split}\|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)|f(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t\\
&\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma^{-1}(w)|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\end{split}

where we used the change of variable
x\mapsto y=x-tw
to compute the integral over
\mathbb{T}^{d}
. Using then assumption (
1.8
),
we obtain

\left\|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 1.

Notice that, with straightforward computations, one has

(4.9)

\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\right](x,v)\\
=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\left[\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)-1\right]\exp\left(-\left(i\eta+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)f(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t

for almost every
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. In particular,

(4.10)

\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\,\left[1-\frac{\sigma(w)}{\varepsilon+\sigma(w)}\right]\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\,.

This allows to recover (
4.8
)
by a simple use of the dominated convergence theorem.

From the above definition, and with a slight abuse of notations, we set

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}),\qquad\forall\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0

so that
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
is the strong limit of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. One actually has the following regularizing properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
:

Lemma 4.8
.

For any
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}\}
one has

(4.11)

\sup\left\{\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{k})}\;,\;\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0\right\}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{k}\|_{\infty}<\infty.

Proof.

Given
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,
\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0
and
k\geqslant 0
, one has

\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\leqslant\|\mathsf{M}_{0}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}

and it suffices to prove the result for
\mathsf{M}_{0}f
. One checks easily that

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\sigma^{-k}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w)t\right)\mathrm{d}t

i.e.

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)}{\sigma(w)}\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)}{\sigma^{k}(v)}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\vartheta_{k}(w)\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

which proves the result since
\vartheta_{k}\in L^{\infty}(V,\bm{m})
as soon as
k\leqslant N_{0}
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

The same proof shows that, actually,

\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\frac{\sigma(w)}{\mathrm{Re}\lambda+\sigma(w)}\,\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0},

\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
. Thefore,

(4.12)

\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\sup_{w}\frac{\sigma(w)}{\mathrm{Re}\lambda+\sigma(w)}=\frac{\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}{\mathrm{Re}\lambda+\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}

since the mapping
x\mapsto\frac{x}{\mathrm{Re}\lambda+x}
is increasing for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0.

With this, one can prove that the above convergence in (
4.8
) extends to the stronger norm
\mathbb{X}_{k}
for
k\leqslant N_{0}
and to iterations of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})
:

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Then

(4.13)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0,\qquad\forall k\leqslant N_{0}.

Moreover, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}

(4.14)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j}f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0

uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
for any
k\leqslant N_{0}.

Proof.

Recalls that (
4.9
) gives the expression of
\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f
. In particular, for any
k\in\mathbb{R}
,

\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\\
\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\,\left[1-\frac{\sigma(w)}{\varepsilon+\sigma(w)}\right]\left(\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{V}\sigma^{-k}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\right)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\,\left[1-\frac{\sigma(w)}{\varepsilon+\sigma(w)}\right]\vartheta_{k}(w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w).

Thus, for
k\leqslant N_{0}
, since
\vartheta_{k}\in L^{\infty}(V)
, we deduce that

(4.15)

\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{k}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\,\left[1-\frac{\sigma(w)}{\varepsilon+\sigma(w)}\right]\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\,

and recover (
4.13
)
by a simple use of the dominated convergence theorem. Let us prove now (
4.14
) by induction on
j\in\mathbb{N}.
For
j=1
, the result is exactly (
4.8
). Assume the result to be true for
j\geqslant 1
. Given
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, set

g(\varepsilon,\eta)=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f.

Since
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}g(\varepsilon,\eta)=\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f
in
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
then

\left\{g(\varepsilon,\eta)\;;\;\eta\in\mathbb{R}\;;\;\varepsilon\in[0,1]\right\}

is a compact subset of
\mathbb{X}_{0}
We notice now that

\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j+1}f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j+1}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\leqslant\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j}g(\varepsilon,\eta)-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j}g(\varepsilon,\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}\\
+\|\vartheta_{k}\|_{\infty}^{j}\left\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}f\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

where we used (
4.11
). The compactness of the family
\{g(\varepsilon,\eta)\;;\;\eta\in\mathbb{R}\;;\;\varepsilon\in[0,1]\}\subset\mathbb{X}_{0}
together with the induction hypothesis easily gives then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j}g(\varepsilon,\eta)-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j}g(\varepsilon,\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0.

This readily implies that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j+1}f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j+1}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0

which achieves the inductive proof.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

Notice that an easy consequence of (
4.13
) together with Corollary
4.3
is that

(4.16)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k-1}}=0,\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

and any
k\leqslant N_{0}
.

Remark 4.12
.

As in Corollary
4.3
, we deduce in particular that, if
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a given compact interval and

g\>:\>\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\longmapsto g(\lambda)\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
is a continuous mapping such that the limit

\widetilde{g}(\eta):=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}g(\varepsilon+i\eta)

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
uniformly with respect to
\eta\in I
. Then

(4.17)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\eta\in I}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}g(\varepsilon+i\eta)-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\widetilde{g}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0

for any
s\leqslant N_{0}
. As in Remark
4.4
, if
I=\mathbb{R}
, the limit (
4.17
) still holds true under the additional assumption that

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\|g(\varepsilon+i\eta)\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s+1}}=0\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0.

In such a case, one has
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}g(\varepsilon+i\eta)
converges to
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\widetilde{g}(\eta)
in
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{s})
.

4.3.
About the differentiability of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}

Let us now focus on the strong differentiability of the mapping
\lambda\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
at
\lambda=0
. We begin with observing that the above computations all deal with limit of operators related to
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})
for a given
\eta
(i.e. they concern limits along
horizontal lines
) since this is enough for our analysis. Regarding the limiting behaviour around
\lambda=0
, we will need to allow a convergence in the usual sense in
\mathbb{C}
. Namely, we can reformulate our results as follows with the exact same proofs. We collect the results we will need later on in the following

Theorem 4.13
.

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s+1}
,

(4.18)

\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Then

(4.19)

\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathsf{M}_{0}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0,\\
\qquad\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k-1}}=0,\qquad\forall k\leqslant N_{0},

Moreover, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}

(4.20)

\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{j}f-\mathsf{M}_{0}^{j}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}=0

uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
for any
k\leqslant N_{0}.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the ones derived in the previous subsection. For instance, for
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, one has

\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|e^{-\lambda t}-1\right|\,\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}t\,\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

Then, since for any
t\geqslant 0
,
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left|e^{-\lambda t}-1\right|=0
,
\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\left|e^{-\lambda t}-1\right|\leqslant 2
for any
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
and
since
t\mapsto\|U_{0}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}
is integrable over
[0,\infty)
, we deduce from the dominated convergence theorem that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

We conclude then to (
4.18
) as in the proof of (
4.3
). The other results are based upon the same arguments. Details are left to the reader.∎

For the first derivative of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
one has the following
1
1

1

In all the sequel, when considering
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}
, we will always assume that
\lambda
approaches
0
belonging to
\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, i.e.

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\{\ldots\}=\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\{\ldots\}.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, the limit

{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}f

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
and is denoted
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f
. Moreover, one has

\left\|\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}.

Proof.

Given
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, let

\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)f(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t,\qquad(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V.

One has, with the usual change of variables
x\mapsto y=x-tw
and since

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}t\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)}{\sigma^{2}(w)}\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)}{\sigma(w)}

which proves that

\|\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}.

Noticing now that

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)f(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t

one gets that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}f-\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

thanks to the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

We can extend this to higher power of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
under the sole assumption that
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, namely

Proposition 4.15
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, set

\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)=\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{n},\qquad\forall\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0.

Then, for any
p\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
, if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{p}
there exists
\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
for any
s\leqslant N_{0}
such that

\underset{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{p}}\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0

and

(4.21)

\sup_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(\lambda)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}C_{n,p}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{p}},

where

C_{n,p}=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\|\vartheta_{r_{j}}\|_{\infty}

with
\bm{r}=\left(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
is a multi-index such that
\displaystyle|\bm{r}|=\sum_{j=1}^{n}r_{j}=p
and
\displaystyle{p\choose\bm{r}}=\frac{p!}{r_{1}!\ldots r_{n}!}.

The proof of this result is deferred to Appendix
B
.

5.
Spectral properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
along the imaginary axis

Recall that we defined

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}),\qquad\qquad\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0

so that

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\varphi(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\left(\lambda+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\varphi(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t,

for any
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0},
and a.e
(x,v)\in\Omega\times V.

We study here more carefully the properties of
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
for
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. We begin with the following which, as we shall see allow to prove Theorem
1.2
in the Introduction.

Proposition 5.1
.

Assume that
\mathcal{K}
is an irreducible operator satisfying Assumptions
1.1
and the measure
\bm{m}
satisfies (
1.12
). There exists a positive
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
such that

\mathsf{M}_{0}\,\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}(x,v)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=1.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathsf{M}_{0}
is stochastic, power-compact and irreducible. Consequently, its spectral radius
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{0})=1
is an algebraically simple and isolated eigenvalue of
\mathsf{M}_{0}
and there is a normalised and positive eigenfunction
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
such that

\mathsf{M}_{0}\,\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=1.

In particular, one can define

(5.1)

\Psi(x,v)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\,\exp\left(-s\sigma(v)\right)\varphi_{0}(x-sv,v)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\forall(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V

and checks easily that

\|\Psi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}=\int_{V}\sigma(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-s\sigma(v)\right)\,\varphi_{0}(x-sv,v)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}(y,v)\,\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\|\varphi_{0}\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

i.e.
\Psi\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
. Then, since
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}})
, one has
\mathcal{K}\Psi\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}},
i.e.
\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}.
Since

\varphi_{0}=\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi_{0},
we deduce that

\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
and this proves the Proposition.∎

The above Proposition as well as its proof allow to prove the existence and uniqueness of the invariant density

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

With the notations of the above proof, recall that we define
\Psi
through (
5.1
) with
\Psi\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}.
Since we prove that
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
one actually has that

\Psi\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}-1}.

Moreover, a simple computation shows that

U_{0}(t)\Psi(x,v)=\exp\left(-t\sigma(v)\right)\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-s\sigma(v)\right)\varphi_{0}(x-(t+s)v,v)\mathrm{d}s\\
=\int_{t}^{\infty}\exp\left(-s\sigma(v)\right)\varphi_{0}(x-sv,v)\mathrm{d}s

so that,

t^{-1}\left(U_{0}(t)\Psi(x,v)-\Psi(x,v)\right)=-\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-s\sigma(v)\right)\varphi_{0}(x-sv,v)\mathrm{d}s

and one sees that
\Psi\in\mathscr{D}(\mathcal{A})
with

\mathcal{A}\Psi=\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}t^{-1}\left(U_{0}(t)\Psi-\Psi\right)=-\varphi_{0}

where the limit is meant in
\mathbb{X}_{0}.
Since
\mathcal{K}\Psi=\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi_{0}
, the identity
\mathsf{M}_{0}\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0}
gives

\left(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}\right){\Psi}=0

i.e.
{\Psi}
is the invariant density of
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. The fact that the invariant density with unit norm is unique comes from the irreducibility of
\left(\mathcal{V}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. Notice that, then since the same result can also be proven in the spatially homogeneous case, i.e. considering only the semigroup generated by
\mathcal{K}
, one sees that there also exists a unique invariant density
\widetilde{\Psi}
to
\mathcal{K}
, i.e.
\widetilde{\Psi}=\widetilde{\Psi}(v)>0
such that
\mathcal{K}\widetilde{\Psi}=0
. In such case of course,
(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\widetilde{\Psi}=0
and, by uniqueness,
\Psi=\widetilde{\Psi}
which proves that
\Psi
turns out to be spatially homogeneous.∎

Remark 5.2
.

Notice that, in the above proof, the definition we gave of
\Psi
is somehow the expression we would deduce from
\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\varphi_{0}
if
0\notin\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A})
. Of course, what happens here is that, because
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, the expression still makes sense. This idea will be the cornerstone of our construction of the trace of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})
along the imaginary axis in Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 5.3
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}
with
\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0
,

r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})<1.

Proof.

We already saw in
(
4.12
) that

\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}{\mathrm{Re}\lambda+\|\sigma_{\infty}\|_{\infty}},\qquad\forall\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0.

In particular, for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0
,
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})<1.
Let us focus on the case
\mathrm{Re}\lambda=0
.
For
\lambda=i\eta
, one has

\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})\qquad\text{ with }\quad\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|\leqslant\mathsf{M}_{0}

where
\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|
denotes the absolute value operator of
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
(see
[
16
]
). The operator
\mathsf{M}_{0}
being power compact, the same holds for
\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|
by a domination argument so that

r_{\mathrm{ess}}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)=0

where
r_{\mathrm{ess}}(\cdot)
denotes the essential spectral radius. We prove that
r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)<1
by contradiction: assume, on the contrary,
r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)=1>r_{\mathrm{ess}}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)=0
, then
r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)
is an isolated eigenvalue of
\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|
with finite algebraic multiplicity and also an eigenvalue of the dual operator, associated to a nonnegative eigenfunction. From the fact that
\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|\leqslant\mathsf{M}_{0}
with
\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|\neq\mathsf{M}_{0}
, one can invoke
[
29
, Theorem 4.3]
to get that

r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)<r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{0})=1

which is a contradiction. Therefore,
r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)<1
and, since
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})\leqslant r_{\sigma}(\left|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\right|)
, the conclusion holds true.
∎

Theorem 5.4
.

If
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
satisfies Assumption
1.1
then
i\mathbb{R}\subset\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}).

Proof.

From Proposition
5.3
and Banach-Steinhaus Theorem
[
8
, Theorem 2.2, p. 32]
, for any
\eta\neq 0
,

(5.2)

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<\infty.

Recall that, for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0
,

(5.3)

\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\,.

Now, combining (
4.11
) and (
4.6
) one has

\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{1}\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty}<\infty

which, thanks to (
5.2
), yields

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<\infty.

This, together with (
4.2
) and (
5.3
) proves that, for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\eta\neq 0
, it holds

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\infty,

whence
i\eta\in\mathfrak{S}(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
for any
\eta\neq 0
. Recalling that
0\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
we get the conclusion.∎

5.1.
Spectral properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
in the vicinity of
\lambda=0
.

We recall that, being
\mathsf{M}_{0}
stochastic power-compact and irreducible, the spectral radius
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{0})=1
is an algebraically simple and isolated eigenvalue of
\mathsf{M}_{0}
and there exists
0<r<1
such that

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{0})\setminus\{1\}\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z|<r\}

and there is a normalised and positive eigenfunction
\varphi_{0}
such that

(5.4)

\mathsf{M}_{0}\,\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0},\qquad\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=1.

Because
\mathsf{M}_{0}
is stochastic, the dual operator
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\star}
(in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V,\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v))
) admits the eigenfunction

\varphi_{0}^{\star}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}

associated to the algebraically simple eigenvalue
1
and the second part of (
5.4
) reads

\langle\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{\star}\rangle=1

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the duality production between
\mathbb{X}_{0}
and its dual
\mathbb{X}^{\star}_{0}
.
Notice that then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n})\setminus\{1\}\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z|<r\}

with

\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n}\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0},\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

The spectral projection of
\mathsf{M}_{0}
associated to the eigenvalue
1
coincide then with that associated to
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\mathsf{n}}
(see Theorem
C.1
), i.e

\mathsf{P}(0)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{0})\mathrm{d}z=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{0}^{n})\mathrm{d}z

where
r_{0}>0
is chosen so that
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z-1|=r_{0}\}\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z|>r\}.

We recall that Theorem
3.1
established the fact that there exists
\mathsf{q}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\left\{\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})

is collectively compact. From now on, we set

\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}=\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}},\qquad 0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1.

A first consequence of this collective compactness and the strong convergence of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
towards
\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
is the following

Lemma 5.5
.

For any
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, there is
0<\delta<\tfrac{1}{2}|\eta_{0}|
such that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Proof.

Notice that the strong convergence

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f=\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f

can be proven for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
thanks to the collective compactness assumption and
[
1
, Theorem 5.3 (d)]
. The fact that the convergence is uniform with respect to
\eta
and that the uniform convergence transfers from
\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})
to
\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})
is deduced as follows.
For the uniform convergence, we closely follow the proof of
[
1
, Theorem 5.3 (d)]
. First, recall that, for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})<1
thanks to Proposition
5.3
. Then, according to
[
1
, Theorem 5.3]
that, for any
\bar{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,

(5.5)

\lim_{\eta\to\bar{\eta}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\bar{\eta}})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\,,

due to the collective compactness of
\{\mathsf{H}_{i\eta},\eta\in\mathbb{R}\}.

Let us consider
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and observe that, if
0<\delta<\tfrac{|\eta_{0}|}{2}
then,
\eta\neq 0
whenever
|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta
.
According to Proposition
5.3
, there is
\varrho\in(0,1)
such that

(5.6)

r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{i\eta_{0}})<\varrho<1.

For
\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta
,

\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}=\left[\mathbf{I}-\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\right]\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)

and, due to the collective compactness of
\left\{\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\;;\;0<\varepsilon<1\right\}
and the strong convergence of
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
to
\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}
as
\lambda\to i\eta_{0}
, we deduce from
[
1
, Lemma 5.2]
that

\lim_{\lambda\to i\eta_{0}}\left\|\bigg[\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\bigg]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=0

and there exist
\varepsilon_{0}>0,\delta>0
such that

\left\|\left[\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\right]^{2}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{1}{2},\qquad\forall\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta,\quad 0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0},\quad|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta.

In particular, for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0},
and
|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta
,
\mathbf{I}-\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})
is invertible and there exists
C>0
such that

\underset{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}{\sup_{0\leqslant\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}}}\left\|\mathcal{R}\big(1,\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\big)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<\infty.

This gives that, for any
\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta
with
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,
|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta
,
\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
is invertible with

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})=\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\mathcal{R}\bigg(1,\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta_{0}})\bigg)

with

\underset{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}{\sup_{0\leqslant\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=M<\infty.

This shows that there exists
R\in(0,1)
such that

r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})<R\qquad\forall\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta,\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),\quad|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta.

Since
r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})=r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})
, we deduce that

r_{\sigma}(\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})<R<1\qquad\forall\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),\quad\eta\in(\eta_{0}-\delta,\eta_{0}+\delta).

Then, from the identity

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})=\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})

we deduce that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

(5.7)

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\left\|\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\\
&\leqslant M\left\|\left(\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta}\right)\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}.\end{split}

Now, according to Lemma
4.10
(see Eq. (
4.14
)), one has

(5.8)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\|\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}g-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta}g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Let now
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. According to (
5.5
), the family

\{g(\eta)=\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f\;;\;\eta\in[\eta_{0}-\delta,\eta_{0}+\delta]\}\quad\text{ is a relatively compact subset of $\mathbb{X}_{0}$}

and we deduce then easily from (
5.8
) that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in[\eta_{0}-\delta,\eta_{0}+\delta]}\left\|\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}g(\eta)-\mathsf{H}_{i\eta}g(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Thanks to (
5.7
), we deduce that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|\leqslant\delta}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{i\eta})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0,\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Recalling that

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f=\sum_{j=0}^{\mathsf{q}-1}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{j}f\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

we deduce from the above convergence of
\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})
and (
4.14
) pointing out that all convergence holds uniformly with respect to
\eta\in[\eta_{0}-\delta,\eta_{0}+\delta]
.
∎

Thanks to Theorem
3.1
, the spectral structure of
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\mathsf{q}}
is inherited by both
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
for
\lambda
small enough. In the sequel, for any
z_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
,
r>0
, the disc of center
z_{0}
and radius
r>0
is denoted:

\mathds{D}(z_{0},r):=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z-z_{0}|<r\}.

One has

Theorem 5.6
.

For any
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
the spectrum of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
is given by

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})=\{0\}\cup\{\mu_{j}(\lambda)\;;\;j\in\mathbb{N}_{\lambda}\subset\mathbb{N}\}

where,
\mathbb{N}_{\lambda}
is a (possibly finite) subset of
\mathbb{N}
and, for each
j\in\mathbb{N}_{\lambda}
,
\mu_{j}(\lambda)
is an isolated eigenvalue of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
of finite algebraic multiplicities and
0
being the only possible accumulation point of the sequence
\{\mu_{j}(\lambda)\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{\lambda}}
. Moreover,

|\mu_{j}(\lambda)|<1\qquad\text{ for any }j\in\mathbb{N}_{\lambda},\qquad\lambda\neq 0.

Finally, there exist
\delta_{0}>0
and
r_{0}\in(0,1)
such that, for any
|\lambda|\leqslant\delta_{0}
,
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
,

(5.9)

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\cap\mathds{D}(1,r_{0})=\{\mu(\lambda)\}

where
\mu(\lambda)
is an algebraically simple eigenvalue of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
such that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mu(\lambda)=1

and there exist an eigenfunction
\varphi_{\lambda}
of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
and an eigenfunction
\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}
of
\left(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)^{\star}
associated to
\mu(\lambda)
such that

\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle=1,\qquad\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{0}\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

and the spectral projection
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
associated to
\mu(\lambda)

(\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+})
is given by

(5.10)

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\psi=\langle\psi,\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle\,\varphi_{\lambda},\qquad\psi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

and is such that

(5.11)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\psi-\mathsf{P}(0)\psi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0,\qquad\forall\psi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
is compact, the structure of
\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})
follows. The fact that all eigenvalues have modulus less than one comes from Proposition
5.3
. This gives the first part of the Proposition. For the second part, the
collective compactness
of
\{\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\;,\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\}
is used in a crucial way. Indeed, recall that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\varphi-\mathsf{H}_{0}\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}}=0,\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}.

This, together with the collective compactness of the family
\{\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\,;\,0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\}
give the separation of the spectrum thanks to
[
1
, Theorem 5.3 and Proposition 6.3]
. Namely, there exist
\delta_{0},r_{0}
small enough such that, for
|\lambda|<\delta_{0}
small enough, the curve
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z-1|=r_{0}\}
is separating the spectrum
\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})
into two disjoint parts, say

\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})=\mathfrak{S}_{\text{in}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\cup\mathfrak{S}_{\text{ext}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})

where
\mathfrak{S}_{\text{in}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;|z-1|<r_{0}\}=\mathds{D}(1,r_{0})
and
\mathfrak{S}_{\text{ext}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\subset\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;|z-1|>r_{0}\}.
Moreover, the spectral projection of
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
associated to
\mathfrak{S}_{\text{in}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})
, defined as,

(5.12)

\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\mathrm{d}z,

is such that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)\varphi-\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(0)\varphi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}=0\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}

and

\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Range}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)))=\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Range}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(0))),\qquad|\lambda|<\delta_{0},\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0.

Now, from the same consideration as those in the beginning of Section
5.1
,

\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Range}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(0)))=1.

Therefore,
\mathrm{dim}(\mathrm{Range}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)))=1
for
|\lambda|<\delta_{0},

\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0,
i.e.

\mathfrak{S}_{\text{in}}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})=\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\cap\mathds{D}(1,r_{0})=\{\nu(\lambda)\}

reduces to some
algebraically simple
eigenvalue of
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
. From Theorem
C.1
(and Remark
C.2
) in Appendix
C
, one deduces that there exists a unique eigenvalue
\mu(\lambda)
of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
such that

\nu(\lambda)=\mu(\lambda)^{\mathsf{q}}\,,

that eigenvalue
\mu(\lambda)
is simple and

\mathfrak{S}_{\text{in}}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})=\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\cap\mathds{D}(1,r_{0})=\{\mu(\lambda)\},\qquad|\lambda|<\delta_{0},\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0.

Notice that, clearly

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mu(\lambda)=1\qquad(\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0)

while, defining

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}\left(z,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\mathrm{d}z,\qquad|\lambda|\leqslant\delta_{0}

one deduces again from Theorem
C.1
that

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)\,,\qquad|\lambda|\leqslant\delta_{0}.

In particular, (
5.11
) holds true.
Setting then

\varphi_{\lambda}:=\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi_{0},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}

where
\varphi_{0}
is the eigenfunction of
\mathsf{M}_{0}
in (
5.4
), one sees that
\varphi_{\lambda}
converges to
\mathsf{P}(0)\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{0}\neq 0
as
\lambda\to 0
. Thus,
\varphi_{\lambda}\neq 0
for
\lambda
small enough and, since
\mu(\lambda)
is algebraically simple,
\varphi_{\lambda}
is an eigenfunction of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
for
|\lambda|
small enough. Setting

\beta_{\lambda}:=\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{0}^{\star}\rangle\neq 0

one sees that
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\beta_{\lambda}=\beta_{0}=\langle\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\star}_{0}\rangle=1

where we recall
\varphi_{0}^{\star}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}
. In particular, for
|\lambda|
small enough,
\beta_{\lambda}\neq 0
and, setting

\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\beta_{\lambda}}\mathsf{P}^{\star}(\lambda)\varphi_{0}^{\star}

one checks easily that

\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\rangle=\beta_{\lambda}^{-1}\langle\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{0}^{\star}\rangle=\beta_{\lambda}^{-1}\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{0}^{\star}\rangle=1

and, since
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
is of rank one, the expression (
5.10
) follows.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

Notice that, in full generality, it is not true that
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\ast}
converges strongly to
\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\ast}
as
\lambda\to 0
in
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\ast}.
In particular, one cannot conclude that
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}
converges to
\varphi_{0}^{\star}
in
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\ast}.
However, from the strong convergence of
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
to
\mathsf{P}(0)
and the convergence of
\varphi_{\lambda}
to
\varphi_{0}
, one deduces that

\begin{split}\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\langle\psi,\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle&=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\beta_{\lambda}^{-1}\langle\psi,\mathsf{P}^{\star}(\lambda)\varphi^{\star}_{0}\rangle\\
&=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\beta_{\lambda}^{-1}\langle\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\psi,\varphi^{\star}_{0}\rangle=\beta_{0}^{-1}\langle\mathsf{P}(0)\psi,\varphi_{0}^{\ast}\rangle=\langle\psi,\mathsf{P}(0)^{\star}\varphi^{\star}_{0}\rangle\end{split}

so that

(5.13)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\langle\psi,\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\psi(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\qquad\forall\psi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

i.e.
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}
converges to
\varphi_{0}^{\star}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}
in the weak-
\ast
topology of
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\star}
as
\lambda\to 0.

We can upgrade the convergence in (
5.11
) as follows

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
,
s\leqslant N_{0}
. Then

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{P}(0)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0.

Proof.

The proof is based upon the following observation: if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s}
and
g(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
with
|z-1|=r_{0}
, then

zg(\lambda)=f+\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}g(\lambda).

Since
\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|g(\lambda)-g(0)\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0
one has, from the regularising effect of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}g(\lambda)-\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}g(0)\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0

as soon as
s\leqslant N_{0}.
This proves (because
f\in\mathbb{X}_{s})
that

g(\lambda)=z^{-1}f+z^{-1}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}g(\lambda)\in\mathbb{X}_{s}

and

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|g(\lambda)-g(0)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0

where the convergence is actually uniform with respect to
z\in\Gamma=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|z-1|=r_{0}\}
since
\sup_{z\in\Gamma}|z|^{-1}<\infty.
Equivalently

(5.14)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sup_{z\in\Gamma}\left\|\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})f-\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{0})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0.

Since

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\Gamma}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})f\mathrm{d}z,\qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\quad|\lambda|\leqslant\delta_{0}

this gives the conclusion.∎

From now, we define
\delta>0
small enough, so that the rectangle

\mathscr{C}_{\delta}:=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant\delta\,,\,|\mathrm{Im}\lambda|\leqslant\delta\}\subset\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\;;\;|\lambda|<\delta_{0}\}\,,

where
\delta_{0}
is introduced in the previous Theorem
5.6
. In the sequel, the notion of differentiability of functions
h\>:\>\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\mapsto h(\lambda)\in Y
(where
Y
is a given Banach space) is the usual one but, if
\mathrm{Re}\lambda=0
, we have to emphasize the fact that limits are always meant in
\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

2
2

2

This means for instance that, if
\lambda_{0}\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
with
\mathrm{Re}\lambda_{0}>0,

h
is differentiable means that it is holomorphic in a neighborhood of
\lambda_{0}
whereas, for
\lambda_{0}=i\eta_{0}
,
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the differentiability at
\lambda_{0}
of
h
at means that there exists
h^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})\in Y
such that

\underset{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to\lambda_{0}}}\left\|\frac{h(\lambda)-h(\lambda_{0})}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}-h^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})\right\|_{Y}=0

where
\|\cdot\|_{Y}
is the norm on
Y
.

Lemma 5.9
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
, we denote with
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
the spectral projection associated to the simple eigenvalue
\mu(\lambda)
of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
as defined in Theorem
5.6
. For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, the mapping

\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\longmapsto\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

is differentiable and

\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)f=-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})f\mathrm{d}z

where
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{q}}^{(1)}(0)
is the derivative of
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
at
\lambda=0
.

Proof.

Recall that, being
\mu(\lambda)
a simple eigenvalue of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
, we deduce from Theorem
C.1
that

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)

where
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)
is the spectral projection associated to
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}=\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}}
and its simple eigenvalue
\mu^{\mathsf{q}}(\lambda)
. One has then, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}

\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f=\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f\mathrm{d}z.

As soon as
z\notin\mathfrak{S}\left(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)
for any
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
, one has

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f=-\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\right)\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f,

so that

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f=-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\right)\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f\mathrm{d}z\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}.

Now, since
\left\{\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\;;\;0\leqslant\mathrm{Re}\lambda\leqslant 1\right\}
is collectively compact and
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}
converges strongly to
\mathsf{H}_{0}
on
\mathbb{X}_{0}
as
\lambda\to 0
, we deduce from

[
1
, Theorem 5.3]
that

(5.15)

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sup_{|z-1|=r_{0}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})g-\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})g\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Now, recall from Lemma
4.15
that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\psi-\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}\psi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

for any
\psi\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
where
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}=\mathsf{L}_{\mathsf{q}}^{(1)}(0)
. Introducing

\psi(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f

which depends on
z\in\mathds{D}(1,r_{0})
, one can argue as in the proof of Corollary
5.8
to deduce that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\psi(\lambda)-\psi(0)\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}=0\qquad s\leqslant N_{0}

where the convergence is uniform with respect to
z\in\mathbb{D}(1,r_{0})
. One deduces then that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sup_{|z-1|=r_{0}}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f-\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

and then, thanks to (
5.15
) again, we deduce that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\sup_{|z-1|=r_{0}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda})f-\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\prime}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{0})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

which proves the differentiability in
0
of
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f
. The same computations also give

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\mathsf{P}(\varepsilon+i\eta)=-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\{|z-1|=r_{0}\}}\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\right)\mathcal{R}(z,\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\mathrm{d}z,\qquad\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

Using now Lemma
B.2
which asserts that
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
converges to
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
uniformly with respect to
\eta
, we can prove as in Lemma
4.15
that the same holds for
\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\mathsf{H}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}
and one deduces the second part of the Lemma.
∎

We deduce from this the differentiability of the simple eigenvalue
\mu(\lambda)

Lemma 5.10
.

With the notations of Theorem
5.6
, the function
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\mapsto\mu(\lambda)\in\mathbb{C}
is differentiable with derivative
\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)
such that the limit

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)=\mu^{\prime}(0)

exists with
\mu^{\prime}(0)<0.

Proof.

Recall that we introduced in the proof of Theorem
5.6
the function
\varphi_{\lambda}=\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi_{0}
as well as the unique eigenfunction
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\in\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\star}
of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\ast}
associated to
\mu(\lambda)
and such that
\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle=1
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the duality bracket between
\mathbb{X}_{0}
and its dual
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\star}.
Since
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, the mapping
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}\mapsto\varphi_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
is differentiable with

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi_{0},\qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

according to Lemma
5.9
. Since

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}=\mu(\lambda)\varphi_{\lambda},

and the mapping
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\mapsto\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
is analytic while
\mu(\lambda)
is a
simple
eigenvalue, we deduce from
[
22
, Chapter II-1]
that, for any
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
, the derivative
\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)
exists with

\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{\lambda}+\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}=\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)\varphi_{\lambda}+\mu(\lambda)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}.

Taking now the duality bracket of this identity with
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}
and noticing that

\left\langle\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda},\mathsf{M}^{\star}_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\right\rangle=\mu(\lambda)\left\langle\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\right\rangle

we get that

\left\langle\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\right\rangle=\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)\langle\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}\rangle=\mu^{\prime}(\lambda).

One sees that

\left|\left\langle\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\right\rangle-\left\langle\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\star}_{0}\right\rangle\right|\\
\leqslant\left|\left\langle\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\left(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{0}\right),\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{0}-\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\right\rangle\right|\\
+\left|\left\langle\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}-\varphi^{\star}_{0}\right\rangle\right|

where the first and second terms on the right-hand side converges to zero as
\lambda\to 0
since

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\|\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{0}\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}=0\qquad\text{ and}\quad\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{0}-\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0}\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

(recall that
\varphi_{0}\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
) while the third term goes to zero as
\lambda\to 0
since
\varphi_{\lambda}^{\star}
converges to
\varphi_{0}^{\star}
in the weak-
\star
topology of
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{\star}.
We deduce from this that

\mu^{\prime}(0)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\mu^{\prime}(\lambda)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\langle\left(\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\varphi_{\lambda},\varphi^{\star}_{\lambda}\right\rangle=\langle\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0},\varphi_{0}^{\ast}\rangle.

Therefore, using Lemma
4.15
(see in particular (
B.1
)) and since
\varphi_{0}^{\ast}=1
, we deduce that

\begin{split}\mu^{\prime}(0)&=\int_{\Omega\times V}\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\prime}\varphi_{0}(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&=-\int_{\Omega\times V}\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}\,t\exp\left(-\sigma(w)t\right)\,\varphi_{0}\left(x-tw,w\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}(y,w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}t\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\\
&=-\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varphi_{0}(y,w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\sigma^{-2}(w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\end{split}

i.e.

\mu^{\prime}(0)=-\int_{\Omega\times V}\varphi_{0}(y,w)\sigma^{-1}(w)\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

and the negativity of
\mu^{\prime}(0)
follows since
\varphi_{0}>0
.
∎

We deduce from this the following fundamental result for our construction of the strong limit of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
at
0
,

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
be such that
\mathsf{P}(0)f=0
. Then,

{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\,\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f,

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
and is denoted by
\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f
. Moreover,

\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f=-\frac{1}{\mu^{\prime}(0)}\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)f.

Proof.

Recall that, since
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, the derivative
\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)f
is well-defined whereas, as observed,
\mu^{\prime}(0)\neq 0.
We simply observes then that, given
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
,

\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\right)\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi=\left(1-\mu(\lambda)\right)\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

which implies obviously that

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi=\frac{1}{1-\mu(\lambda)}\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\varphi,\qquad\forall\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

provided
\lambda\neq 0
so that
\mu(\lambda)\neq 1
(and in particular
1\notin\mathfrak{S}(\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})
). Then, for
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
with
\mathsf{P}(0)f=0
one can write, for
|\lambda|
small enough,

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f=\frac{1}{1-\mu(\lambda)}\left(\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{P}(0)f\right)=\frac{\lambda}{1-\mu(\lambda)}\left(\frac{\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{P}(0)}{\lambda}\right)\,.

Now, because
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
,

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\frac{\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{P}(0)}{\lambda}=\mathsf{P}^{\prime}(0)f

where the convergence holds in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
while, since
\mu^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
,

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\frac{\lambda}{1-\mu(\lambda)}=-\frac{1}{\mu^{\prime}(0)}

which gives the result.∎

6.
The extension of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
to the imaginary axis

6.1.
Existence of the boundary function for
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})

We begin with the extension of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
far away from zero:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\eta_{0}\neq 0
be given. Then, there is
\delta>0
(small enough) such that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|\leqslant\delta}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\bigg[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\bigg]\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Proof.

We recall that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, Lemma
5.5
established that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})g-\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})g\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

holds for any
g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Recalling that (
4.17
)
holds in particular for
s=1
, i.e.

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}\bigg\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})g-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})g\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}=0,\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

we deduce from Corollary
4.3
that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|<\delta}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Combining this with (
4.3
)
and the fact that

\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f

we deduce the result.
∎

This shows that, away from zero, it is possible to extend the resolvent of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f
along the imaginary axis, i.e. for
\lambda=i\eta
. The extension for
\lambda=0
is much more involved and follows the approach of
[
27
, Section 4]
. For such a case, recalling that, with the definition of
\mathsf{P}_{0}
and
\mathbf{P}
, given
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,

\mathsf{P}(0)f=0\quad\iff\mathbf{P}f=0\quad\iff\varrho_{f}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}f(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=0.

We introduce then

\mathbb{X}_{k}^{0}:=\{f\in\mathbb{X}_{k}\;;\;\varrho_{f}=0\},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}.

which is a closed subspace of
\mathbb{X}_{k}
. Notice that, endowed with the
\mathbb{X}_{k}
-norm,
\mathbb{X}_{k}^{0}
is a Banach space.
Since the semigroup
(\mathcal{V}(t))_{t\geqslant 0}
generated by
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K}
is conservative:

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\mathcal{V}(t)f\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}f\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v),\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0,\quad f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

one has

\begin{split}\int_{\Omega\times V}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)&=\int_{\Omega\times V}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda\,t}V(t)f\mathrm{d}t\right)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&=\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\Omega\times V}f\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v),\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}\end{split}

and therefore the resolvent and all its iterates
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})^{k}
leave
\mathbb{X}_{0}^{0}
invariant (
k\geqslant 0
).
We deduce from Proposition
5.11
the following

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
with
\varrho_{f}=0
, i.e.
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0},
then

{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\,\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f,

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. We denote it by
\mathcal{R}(0)f
and has

\mathcal{R}(0)f=\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\right]\,

Proof.

With the previous notations, if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
, using the fact that
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
and
\mathsf{P}(\lambda)
commute, one has

(6.1)

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}&(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})f\\
&=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)f+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f\\
&=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right)f+\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f\,.\end{split}

We investigate separately the convergence of each of the last three terms. First, since
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
,

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Now, for the second term, recall that
\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}=\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{\mathsf{q}}
. Since
\left\{\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right\}_{\mathrm{Re}\lambda\in[0,1]}
is collectively compact and strongly converges to
\mathsf{H}_{0}(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0))
as
\lambda\to 0
with
r_{\sigma}\left(\mathsf{H}_{0}(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0))\right)<1
, we deduce from the convergence of the spectrum established in
[
1
, Theorem 5.3(a)]
that, for
\delta>0
small enough, there is
c\in(0,1)
such that

r_{\sigma}\left(\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right)\leqslant c<1,\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{\delta}.

Moreover, we deduce from
[
1
, Theorem 5.3 (d)]
that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right)g-\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)g\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0,\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Arguing exactly as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, we deduce first that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right)g-\mathsf{M}_{0}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)g\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}},\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

and then

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{\lambda}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(\lambda)\right)\right)f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{H}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

The convergence of the third term is dealt with in the same way. Indeed, since
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}
, we deduce from Proposition
5.11
that

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Then, as before,

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{M}_{0}\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}=0

and subsequently

\lim_{\lambda\to 0}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})\mathsf{P}(\lambda)f-\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

We proved the convergence of the three terms in the right-hand-side of (
6.1
) and this shows the result.∎

We deduce from this the following which holds under Assumption
1.1
but
does not resort on
(
1.14
)–(
1.13
) and the fact that
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure (see also Remark
6.4
):

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}
with
\varrho_{f}=0
, i.e.
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0},
then the limit

{\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}}\,\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f,

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. We denote it by
\mathcal{R}(\eta)f
and has

\mathcal{R}(\eta)f=\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,A)f+\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f,\qquad&\text{ if }\quad\eta\neq 0\\
\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}\left[\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\right]\,\qquad&\text{ if }\quad\eta=0\,.\end{cases}

Moreover, the convergence of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f
to
\mathcal{R}(\eta)f
is uniform with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The convergence of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f
towards the desired limit is just the combination of the two previous Lemmas. We only need to check the uniform convergence in the vicinity of
\eta=0
and near infinity. Let us first prove that the convergence is uniform with respect to
|\eta|\leqslant\delta.
According to (
4.17
)
and Corollary
4.3
, we only need to prove that the convergence in
\mathbb{X}_{0}

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f=\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)

is uniform with respect to
|\eta|<\delta
where

\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\qquad&\text{ if }\eta\neq 0\\
\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f\qquad&\text{ if }\eta=0.\end{cases}

We argue by contradiction, assuming that there exist
c>0
, a sequence
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n}\subset(0,\infty)
converging to
0
and a sequence
(\eta_{n})_{n}\subset(-\delta,\delta)
such that

(6.2)

\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon_{n}+i\eta_{n}})-\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta_{n})\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\geqslant c>0.

Up to considering a subsequence, if necessary, we can assume without loss of generality that
\lim_{n}\eta_{n}=\eta_{0}
with
|\eta_{0}|\leqslant\delta.
First, one sees that then
\eta_{0}=0
since the convergence of
\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f
to
\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)
is actually uniform in any neighbourhood around
\eta_{0}\neq 0
(see Lemma
5.5
). Because
\eta_{0}=0
, defining
\lambda_{n}:=\varepsilon_{n}+i\eta_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the sequence
(\lambda_{n})_{n}\subset\mathscr{C}_{\delta}
is converging to
0
. Now, from Lemma
6.2
one has

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda_{n}})f=\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f

as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, one also has

\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta_{n})=\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta_{n}})f

also converges as
n\to\infty
towards
\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)f+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}f
still thanks to Lemma
6.2
(recall the convergence holds for
\lambda\to 0
,
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
including the case of purely imaginary
\lambda
). This contradicts (
6.2
). The uniform convergence in the vicinity of
\eta=0
together with Lemma
6.1
, we deduce that, for any
\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, there is
\delta>0
such that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{|\eta-\eta_{0}|\leqslant\delta}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)f\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

To prove that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)f\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

we only to check that there is
R>0
(large enough) such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{|\eta|>R}\bigg\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)f\bigg\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

One observes that, for
|\eta|>R>0
,

\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)f=\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right]\\
+\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\\
+\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\right]

where the two first terms converge to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
uniformly with respect to
|\eta|>R
according to Eq. (
4.3
) in Proposition
4.1
. To prove the result, we only need to prove that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{|\eta|>R}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\right]\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

which is deduced from Remark
4.4
since one checks easily that

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}=0.

This proves the convergence is uniform with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}
, the above Theorem asserts that the resolvent of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f
admits a trace
\mathcal{R}(\eta)f
on the boundary
\lambda=i\eta
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\eta\mapsto\mathcal{R}(\eta)f
belonging to
\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
and, in particular, to
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. Therefore, according to Ingham’s theorem (see e.g.
[
17
, Theorem 1.1]
), we directly deduce that
\mathcal{V}(t)f\to 0
as
t\to\infty
for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}
which, in turns, implies that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\|\mathcal{V}(t)f-\varrho_{f}\Psi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}.

By a density argument, the above still holds for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, providing a
new non quantitative convergence
to equilibrium under the sole assumption of collective compactness in Theorem
2.4
. Notice here indeed that, up to now, we did not use the quantitative estimate (
2.11
) which is the only one in our analysis which requires assumptions (
1.14
)–(
1.13
) and the fact that
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure.

Again, under additional integrability of
f
, one can upgrade the convergence as follows

Corollary 6.5
.

Let
N\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N}^{0},
then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N-1}}=0.

Proof.

For
N=1,
the result is nothing but Theorem
6.3
. For
N\geqslant 1
