, the proof is similar and based upon the representation

\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f+\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f,\qquad\varepsilon>0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

First, for
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k-1}}=0.

We saw in the previous proof that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

as soon as
f\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}.
According to Remark
4.12
(see Eq. (
4.17
)), this implies that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N}}=0

for any
N\leqslant N_{0}
. Then, according to Remark
4.4

(6.3)

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N-1}}=0

and the result follows since
\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)=\mathcal{R}(\eta)f-\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f.

∎

6.2.
Regularity of the boundary function

The previous results ensure that the extension
\mathcal{R}(\eta)
of
\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})
to the imaginary axis
\lambda=i\eta
is continuous and tends to zero at infinity, namely,

\mathcal{R}(\cdot)f\in\mathscr{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{k-1}),\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{k}^{0},\qquad k=1,\ldots,N_{0}.

We need to extend this regularity to capture some differentiability property. The key point is the well-known formula for the resolvent: for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\varepsilon>0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=(-i)^{k}k!\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k+1}f.

In particular, from Lemma
4.5
, we also recall that, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
, the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{k}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. To prove then that the same holds for the boundary function of
\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f
we need to investigate iterates of the resolvent. To do so, we recall the notation introduced in (
A.13
), for
\varepsilon>0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathscr{S}_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{k}=\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{n}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})

so that

\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})=\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})+\mathscr{S}_{0}(\varepsilon+i\eta).

According to (
6.3
), for any
j\leqslant N_{0}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\mathscr{S}_{0}(\varepsilon+i\eta)g-\mathscr{S}_{0}(i\eta)g\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{j-1}}=0,\qquad\forall g\in\mathbb{X}_{1}^{0}

where

\mathscr{S}_{0}(i\eta)=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathsf{\Psi}(\eta)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})g\quad\text{ if }\eta\neq 0\\
\mathcal{R}(0,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{0}\mathcal{R}\left(1,\mathsf{M}_{0}\left(\mathbf{I}-\mathsf{P}(0)\right)\right)g+\mathsf{\Phi}_{0}g\quad\text{ if }\eta=0\end{cases}

One has then the following

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
N\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N}^{0}
and any
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, one has

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N-k}}=0

where
\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\in\mathbb{X}_{N-k}^{0}
for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
.

In particular, if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}^{0}
then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f-\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}-k}}=0,\qquad\forall k\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}

Proof.

Let
N\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}\}
be given. The proof is made by induction over
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
. For
k=1
, the result is exactly Corollary
6.5
. Let us assume that the result is true for
k\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}
and let us prove it for
k+1
. As part of the induction assumption, one has

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=0.

Observe that

\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k+1}f=\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f\\
+\mathscr{S}_{0}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f\,.

According to the induction hypothesis,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f=\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f

holds in
\mathbb{X}_{N-k}
uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. Thanks to Corollary
4.3
(and since
N-k\geqslant 1
),

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f

holds in
\mathbb{X}_{N-k-1}=\mathbb{X}_{N-(k+1)}
uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}.
Now, since
\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\in\mathbb{X}_{N-k}^{0}
by induction assumption, we can resume the proof of Corollary
4.3
together with (
6.3
) to deduce also that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathscr{S}_{0}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k}f=\mathscr{S}_{0}(i\eta)\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f

holds true in
\mathbb{X}_{N-k-1}
uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. We deduce that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k+1}f-\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{k+1}f-\mathscr{S}_{0}(i\eta)\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N-k-1}}=0

which proves the result with

\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k+1}f=\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{k+1}f-\mathscr{S}_{0}(i\eta)\mathcal{R}(\eta)^{k}f.

This achieves the induction and proves the Lemma.
∎

A fundamental consequence is

Corollary 6.7
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}^{0},
the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\mathcal{R}(\eta)f

defined in Theorem
6.3
belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
and the convergence

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=\mathcal{R}(\eta)f

holds in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0}).

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}^{0}
be fixed. Since, for any
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
and any
\varepsilon>0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=(-i)^{k}k!\left[\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})\right]^{k+1}f

the result follows directly from Lemma
6.6
where the derivatives of
\mathcal{R}(\eta)f
are defined by

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathcal{R}(i\eta)f=(-i)^{k}k!\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta)\right]^{k+1}f

for any
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}.

∎

7.
The boundary function of
\mathscr{S}_{n}(\lambda)

This section is devoted to the construction of the trace along the imaginary axis, that is when
\lambda=i\eta
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, of

\mathscr{S}_{n}(\lambda)f=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{n}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})f,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\mathrm{Re}\lambda\geqslant 0,\>n\in\mathbb{N}

for a suitable class of function
f
. Recall that
\mathscr{S}_{n}(\lambda)
has been introduced in (
A.13
). Of course, the crucial observation is the alternative representation of
\mathscr{S}_{n}(\lambda)f
as

(7.1)

\mathscr{S}_{n}(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})-\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})-\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{k}\,\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}

We already investigated the existence and regularity of the traces on the imaginary axis of the first two terms in the right-hand-side of (
7.1
) so we just need to focus on the properties of the
finite sum

(7.2)

{s}_{n}(\lambda):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{k}\ \qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

All the above results allow to prove the regularity the
finite
sum
s_{n}(\lambda)
defined by (
7.2
), the proof of which is deferred to Appendix
B
:

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
be fixed. For any
\varepsilon>0
, the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}\quad\text{ belongs to }\mathscr{C}^{N_{0}-1}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\qquad k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}

exist uniformly with respect to
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. In particular, the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto s_{n}(i\eta)f:=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

belongs to
\mathscr{C}^{N_{0}-1}_{0}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0}).

We have all the tools to prove the first point in Theorem
2.6

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
be such that

(7.3)

\varrho_{f}=\int_{\Omega\times V}f(x,v)\mathrm{d}x\otimes\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=0.

Then, for any
n\geqslant 0
the limit

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathscr{S}_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f,

exists in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. Its limit is denoted
{\Upsilon}_{n}(\eta)f
.

Proof.

We know from Corollary
6.7
that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f

holds in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0}).
In the same way, Lemma
4.5
shows that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f

holds in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. Since one sees easily from Lemma
7.1
that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f=s_{n}(i\eta)f\qquad\text{ in }\quad\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})

we get the result from the representation (
7.1
) which asserts that
\mathscr{S}_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{K})f-\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f-s_{n-1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f
.
∎

In the following, we show also that, if
n
is large enough, the boundary function
\Upsilon_{n}(\cdot)f
and its derivatives are integrable which proves the second point of Theorem
2.6

Proposition 7.3
.

Assume that
n\geqslant 5\cdot 2^{N_{0}-1}
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}^{0}
. Then, the derivatives of the trace function

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

are integrable, i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty\qquad\forall k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}.

Proof.

Let
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
be given as well as
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}^{0}.
Using the continuity of
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f
, it is clear that the mapping

\eta\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f

is locally integrable. It is enough then to prove that there is
R>0
such that

(7.4)

\int_{|\eta|>R}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty\qquad\forall k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}.

We recall that, for any
\mathsf{p}>4
,

\int_{|\eta|>1}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{\mathsf{p}}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta<\infty.

We
fix

\mathsf{p}>4
in all the rest of the proof. We recall that

\mathsf{G}_{m}(\lambda)=\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{m},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\qquad m\in\mathbb{N}

and we choose
R>0
large enough so that

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\qquad\text{ for }|\eta|>R.

For
|\eta|>R
, we use the following representation of
\Upsilon_{N}(\eta)f
:

\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{n}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f,\qquad|\eta|>R.

Writing,

\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{j}f=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{m\mathsf{p}+r}f

we have

\begin{split}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f&=\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}^{n}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{i\eta})f=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\right]^{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}f\\
&=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\\
&=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\mathsf{G}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\,.\end{split}

Let us fix then
|\eta|>R
. Thanks to Leibniz rule

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f=(-i)^{k}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}{k\choose j}\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\left[\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k-j}}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right]

so that

\begin{split}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}{k\choose j}\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k-j}}\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\\
&\leqslant\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}{k\choose j}(k-j)!\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k-j+1}}\end{split}

where we used (
4.7
). Since
\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k-j+1}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}
, we get

(7.5)

\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

We use now Lemma
B.5
(see Eq. (
B.6
)) to estimate, given
m,r,n

\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{j0})}\leqslant\bar{C}_{j}\left(m\mathsf{p}+r+n\right)^{j}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m\mathsf{p}+r+n-j}{2^{j}}\rfloor}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

Since
j\leqslant k
and
r\leqslant\mathsf{p-1}
, setting
\bm{C}_{k}=\max_{j\leqslant k}\bar{C}_{j}
, one has

\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\bm{C}_{k}\left((m+1)\mathsf{p}+n\right)^{k}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m\mathsf{p}+r+n-j}{2^{j}}\rfloor}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

Then, since
n\geqslant 2^{k}\mathsf{p}+k\geqslant 2^{j}\mathsf{p}+j
, one has
\lfloor\frac{m\mathsf{p}+r+n-j}{2^{j}}\rfloor\geqslant\mathsf{p}+\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor\mathsf{p}
, i.e.

\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m\mathsf{p}+r+n-j}{2^{j}}\rfloor}(i\eta)=\mathsf{G}_{b}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)

for some
b\geqslant 0
, and we deduce from Lemma
B.4
that

\begin{split}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{m\mathsf{p}+r+n}^{(j)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}&\leqslant\bm{C}_{k}\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{{1}}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\left((m+1)\mathsf{p}+n\right)^{k}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
&\leqslant\bm{C}_{k}\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{{1}}\|_{\infty}\|\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\left((m+1)\mathsf{p}+n\right)^{k}2^{-\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor}\end{split}

since
\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)=\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)^{\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor}
and we choose
R>0
such that
\|\mathsf{G}_{p}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
for
|\eta|>R
.
Noticing that

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=0}^{\mathsf{p}-1}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left((m+1)\mathsf{p}+n\right)^{k}2^{-\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{j}}\rfloor}\leqslant\mathsf{p}(k+1)\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left((m+1)\mathsf{p}+n\right)^{k}2^{-\lfloor\frac{m}{2^{k}}\rfloor}<\infty

we deduce from (
7.5
) that there is a positive constant
\alpha_{k}(n)>0
such that

(7.6)

\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\Upsilon_{n}(\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\alpha_{k}(n)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\mathsf{p}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\qquad\forall|\eta|>R.

We deduce then (
7.4
) thanks to (
2.12
).
∎

We have all in hands to give the full proof of Theorem
2.6
from which, as pointed out in Section
2
, our main convergence Theorem
1.7
is deduced.

Proof of Theorem
2.6
.

The previous two propositions give a complete proof of points
(1)
and
(2)
of Theorem
2.6
. In turns, recalling that (see Proposition
A.5
in Appendix
A
)

(7.7)

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f=\frac{\exp(\varepsilon t)}{2\pi}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta,\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

for any
t>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, we deduce from the uniform convergence obtained in Proposition
7.2
together with the integrability condition in Proposition
7.3
that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
and any
t\geqslant 0

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left((\varepsilon+i\eta)t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\Upsilon_{n+1}(\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta

where the convergence occurs in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
as soon as
n+1\geqslant 5\cdot 2^{N_{0}-1}
thanks to the dominated convergence theorem. This, together with (
A.14
) shows (
2.16
). The proof of (
2.17
)
is then deduced easily after
N_{0}-1
integration by parts, using again Proposition
7.3
. This achieves the proof.∎

Appendix A
Properties of the Dyson-Phillips iterated

A.1.
Continuous dependence with respect to
\mathcal{K}

We begin with recalling that the Dyson-Phillips iterated are depending continuously of
\mathcal{K}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
. We can be more precise here. Namely, let us consider a sequence
\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of boundary operators

\left(\mathcal{K}_{n}\right)_{n}\subset\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0}),\qquad\|\mathcal{K}_{n}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 1,\qquad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}

and introduce
V_{0}(t)=U_{0}(t)
and

(A.1)

V_{n+1}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}V_{n}(t-s)\mathcal{K}_{n+1}U_{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad t\geqslant 0,\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Then

Proposition A.1
.

For any
n\geqslant 1
and any
t\geqslant 0
,
{V}_{n}(t)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
with

(A.2)

\left\|{V}_{n}(t)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left\|\mathcal{K}_{j}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})},\qquad n\geqslant 1,\qquad t\geqslant 0.

Proof.

The proof is made by induction. Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
and
t\geqslant 0
be fixed. For
n=1
, one has

\|{V}_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{0}(t-s)\mathcal{K}_{1}U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathcal{K}_{1}U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}s

since
U_{0}(t)
is a contraction in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Then, one deduces

\|V_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}_{1}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}\mathrm{d}s.

Since
U_{0}(s)
commutes with
\varpi_{-1}(v)=\min\left(1,\sigma(v)\right)
, one has

\|U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\varpi_{-1}(v)e^{-s\sigma(v)}|f(x,v)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)

and, since
\varpi_{-1}\leqslant\sigma
,

\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(x,v)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(v)e^{-s\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}s\\
=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(x,v)|\left(1-e^{-t\sigma(v)}\right)\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\leqslant\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

This proves (
A.2
) for
n=1
. Assume then the result to be true for
n\geqslant 1
and let us prove for
n+1
. One has, as before,

\begin{split}\left\|{V}_{n+1}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|V_{n}(t-s)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathcal{K}_{n+1}U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leqslant\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|V_{n}(t-s)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathcal{K}_{n+1}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\int_{0}^{t}\|U_{0}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

We saw how to estimate this last integral and, with the induction hypothesis
\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|V_{n}(t-s)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|\mathcal{K}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}
, we deduce that

\|V_{n+1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\|\mathcal{K}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

which proves the result.
∎

A.2.
Decay of the iterates

We extend the decay of the semigroup
\left(U_{0}(t))\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
obtained in Lemma
2.1
to the iterates

\left(U_{k}(t)\right)_{t\geqslant 0}
. We recall that, for any
\delta>0
, we introduce

\Lambda_{\delta}:=\{v\in V\;;\;\sigma(v)\geqslant\delta\},\qquad\Sigma_{\delta}=V\setminus\Lambda_{\delta}

and

\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
given by (
2.5
), i.e.

\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}f(x,v)=\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\mathcal{K}f(x,v)\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0},\quad(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V

We prove here Lemma
2.2
which investigate the size of
\|\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Introduce

(A.3)

\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}=\mathcal{K}-\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}

so that

\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}f(x,v)=\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\delta}}(v)\mathcal{K}f(x,v).

Then, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{-1}
, one has, for any
n\geqslant 0
,

\begin{split}\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{\Sigma_{\delta}}|\mathcal{K}f(x,v)|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\mathrm{d}x\int_{V}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\delta}}(v)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)|f(x,w)|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
&\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(x,w)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\delta}}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\frac{\sigma^{n}(v)}{\sigma^{n}(v)}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
&\leqslant\delta^{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(x,w)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\sigma^{-n}(v)\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v).\end{split}

By definition of
\vartheta_{n}
, we deduce that

\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\delta^{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(w)\vartheta_{n}(w)\,|f(x,w)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

i.e.

\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\delta^{n}\|\vartheta_{n}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma(w)\,|f(x,w)|\mathrm{d}x\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\leqslant\delta^{n}\|\vartheta_{n}\|_{\infty}\,\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{-1}}

as soon as
\vartheta_{n}\in L^{\infty}(V)
. This gives (
2.7
).∎

Before proving the precise decay of
U_{k}(t)
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for the clarity of exposition, we give full details for the decay of
U_{1}(t)
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
. Then, there exists some universal constant
C_{1}>0
(depending only on
\mathcal{K}
,
N_{0}
but not on
f
) such that

\left\|U_{1}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{1}\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{N_{0}}\,\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}},\qquad\forall t>0.

Proof.

The proof is based upon the decomposition of
\mathcal{K}
for small and large collision frequency. Namely, for some
\delta>0
to be determined, we use the above splitting

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}+\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}

where
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
is defined in (
2.5
) and

{U}_{1}(t)=U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)+\overline{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t),\qquad t>0,\qquad\delta>0

where
U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t),\overline{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)
are given by

U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{0}(t-s)\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}U_{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\overline{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}U_{0}(t-s)\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}U_{0}(s)\mathrm{d}s

Let now fix
k\geqslant 1,

f\in\mathbb{X}_{k},t>0.

\|U_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|\overline{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

where we used (
A.2
). Using now (
2.7
),

(A.4)

\|U_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\delta^{N_{0}}\|\vartheta_{N_{0}}\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|{U}_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\qquad\forall\delta>0,\qquad t\geqslant 0.

Let us focus then on the estimate for
\ {U_{1}^{(\delta)}}(t)f
. Given
s\in[0,t]
, one computes easily

U_{0}(t-s)\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}U_{0}(s)f(x,v)=\\
\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}(v)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)-s\sigma(w)\right)f(x-tv+s(v-w),w)\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

so that

\|U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\Lambda_{\delta}}\mathrm{d}x\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)-s\sigma(w)\right)\left|f(x-tv+s(v-w),w)\right|\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,w)|\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)-s\sigma(w)\right)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v).

Introducing again

g(x,v)=\sigma(v)^{-k}f(x,v)

and using Fubini’s Theorem, we get

(A.5)

\|U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma^{k}(w)\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)|g(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

where

\begin{split}\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)&=\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)-s\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\exp\left(-t\sigma(v)\right)\bm{k}(v,w)\frac{\exp\left(t\left[\sigma(v)-\sigma(w)\right]\right)-1}{\sigma(v)-\sigma(w)}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\end{split}

(where we notice that, if
\sigma(v)=\sigma(w)
the last quotient is equal to
1
). Notice that, since
\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)-s\sigma(w)\right)\leqslant\exp(-s\sigma(w))
for any
v,w
and any
s\in[0,t]
we have,

\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-s\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s\leqslant\frac{1}{\sigma(w)}\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=1

for any
t\geqslant 0
,
w\in V.

To estimate the integral in the right-hand-side of (
A.5
), we estimate
\Theta_{1}^{\delta}(t,w)
distinguishing between the two cases

w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}\quad\text{ or }\quad w\notin\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}.

Assume first that
w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}
, i.e.
\sigma(w)\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}
. Then, for any
v\in V
,
\sigma(v)-\sigma(w)\leqslant\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}
and, since the mapping

u\in\mathbb{R}\longmapsto\frac{e^{u}-1}{u}

is nondecreasing, we get that

\frac{\exp\left(t\left[\sigma(v)-\sigma(w)\right]\right)-1}{\sigma(v)-\sigma(w)}\leqslant\frac{\exp\left(t\left[\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}\right]\right)-1}{\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}}.

i.e.

\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\frac{\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)-\exp\left(-t\sigma(v)\right)}{\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v),\qquad w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}.

Now, for
v\in\Lambda_{\delta}
,

\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\frac{\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)-\exp\left(-t\sigma(v)\right)}{\sigma(v)-\frac{\delta}{2}}\leqslant\frac{2}{\delta}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)

from which we get

(A.6)

\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant\frac{2\sigma(w)}{\delta}\exp\left(-\frac{\delta}{2}\right),\qquad w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}

where we used that
\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\leqslant\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)=\sigma(w)
. Inserting this into (
A.5
) we deduce

\|U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{2}{\delta}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\sigma^{k+1}(w)|g(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
+\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}^{c}}\sigma^{k}(w)\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\left|g(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w).

For
w\notin\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}
, i.e.
\sigma(w)\leqslant\frac{\delta}{2}
, we simply recall that
\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant 1
and of course
\sigma^{k}(w)\leqslant\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}
so that

\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}^{c}}\sigma^{k}(w)\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\left|g(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\leqslant\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

which, combined with the previous estimate gives

\|U_{1}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{2}{\delta}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\|\sigma^{k+1}g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\qquad\forall\delta>0.

Adding this to (
A.4
) and with
k=N_{0}
, we get

\|U_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\frac{2}{\delta}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\|\sigma^{k+1}g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{N_{0}}\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\delta^{N_{0}}\|\vartheta_{N_{0}}\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}},\qquad\delta>0.

Observing that

\|\sigma^{k+1}g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=\|\sigma\,f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|\sigma^{-k}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\left(1+\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k}}

and there is a positive constant
C>0
such that

\|U_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{N_{0}}\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}},\qquad\forall\delta>0.

Choosing, for
t>e
,

\delta=2(N_{0}+1)\frac{\log t}{t}

we deduce that there is
C_{0}>0
such that

\begin{split}\|U_{1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}&\leqslant C\left(\frac{1}{(N_{0}+1)t^{N_{0}}\log t}+(N_{0}+1)^{N_{0}}\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{N_{0}}\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}\\
&\leqslant C_{0}\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{N_{0}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}},\qquad t>e\end{split}

which gives the result.
∎

We generalise this approach to the other iterates

Lemma A.3
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}
. For any
n\geqslant 1
there exists some universal constant
C_{n}>0
(depending only on
\mathcal{K}
,
n,N_{0}
but not on
f
) such that

\left\|U_{n}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{n}\left(\frac{\log t}{t}\right)^{N_{0}}\,\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}},\qquad\forall t>0.

Proof.

The proof uses the same ideas introduced in the proof for
n=1
. Given
\delta>0
, we still introduce the splitting
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}+\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}
which gives, by a simple combinatorial argument that, for
n\geqslant 1
, one can write

U_{n}(t)=U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)+\overline{U}_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)

where
U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)
is constructed as a Dyson-Phillips iterated involving
only
the operator
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
whereas the reminder term
\overline{U}_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)
is the some of
2^{n}-1
operators

\overline{U}_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{2^{n}-1}\bm{V}_{n}^{(j)}(t)

where, for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,2^{n}-1\}
,
\bm{V}_{n}^{(j)}(t)
is defined by (
A.1
) for a (finite) family of operators
(\mathcal{K}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{K}_{n})
where there is at least one
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
\mathcal{K}_{i}=\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}
(the other ones being indifferently
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
or
\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}
). Using Proposition
A.1
, and recalling that
\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 1
one has then

\|\bm{V}_{n}^{(j)}(t)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

Therefore

\left\|\overline{U}_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\left(2^{n}-1\right)\|\overline{\mathcal{K}}^{(\delta)}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{-1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

and

(A.7)

\left\|U_{n}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\|U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+C(2^{n}-1)\,\delta^{N_{0}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}},\qquad t>0,\qquad\delta>0

where we used (
2.7
)
. We focus now on the expression of
U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f
. From the subsequent Lemma, we have

(A.8)

\|U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\,|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

where
\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)
is defined by

(A.9)

\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)=\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bm{k}(v_{j},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{n})\\
\int_{\Delta_{t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(-(s_{j-1}-s_{j})\sigma(v_{j})\right)\exp\left(-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}s_{n}.

where we used the convention
s_{0}=t
and
\Delta_{t}
denotes the simplex

\Delta_{t}=\left\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\;;\;0\leqslant s_{n}\leqslant s_{n-1}\leqslant\ldots s_{1}\leqslant t\right\}.

We saw in Lemma
A.2
that

\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(s,w)\leqslant\dfrac{2\sigma(w)}{\delta}\exp\left(-s\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\qquad\text{ for }w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}

and
\Theta_{1}^{(\delta)}(s,w)\leqslant 1
for any
s\geqslant 0,w\in V.
Let us prove a similar estimate holds true for any
n\in\mathbb{N}.
The fact that

(A.10)

\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant 1\qquad\text{ for any $t\geqslant 0$ and any $w\in V$}

is easily seen by induction since

\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)=\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)\right)\Theta_{n-1}^{(\delta)}(s,w)\mathrm{d}s.

Let now
w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}
be fixed. We estimate, for
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\Lambda_{\delta}^{n}
the integral on the simplex
\Delta_{t}
as in the previous Lemma starting from the integral with respect to
s_{n}
. For fixed
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-1})
we have, as in Lemma
A.2

\begin{split}G_{1}(s_{n-1},v_{n},w)&:=\int_{0}^{s_{n-1}}\exp\left(-(s_{n-1}-s_{n})\sigma(v_{n})-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{n}\\
&\leqslant\exp\left(-s_{n-1}\sigma(v_{n})\right)\int_{0}^{s_{n-1}}\exp\left(s_{n}\left(\sigma(v_{n})-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{n}\\
&\leqslant\frac{1}{\sigma(v_{n})-\frac{\delta}{2}}\exp\left(-s_{n-1}\frac{\delta}{2}\right).\end{split}

Since
\sigma(v_{n})\geqslant\delta
we get
\sigma(v_{n})-\frac{\delta}{2}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sigma(v_{n})
and

\int_{0}^{s_{n-1}}\exp\left(-(s_{n-1}-s_{n})\sigma(v_{n})-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{n}\leqslant\frac{2}{\sigma(v_{n})}\exp\left(-s_{n-1}\frac{\delta}{2}\right).

Now, for
(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n-2})
given, we multiply this by
\exp\left(-(s_{n-2}-s_{n-1})\sigma(v_{n-1})\right)
and integrate with respect to
s_{n-1}
to get

\begin{split}G_{2}(s_{n-2},v_{n-1},v_{n},w)&:=\int_{0}^{s_{n-2}}\exp\left(-(s_{n-2}-s_{n-1})\sigma(v_{n-1})\right)G(s_{n-1},v_{n},w)\mathrm{d}s_{n-1}\\
&\leqslant\frac{2}{\sigma(v_{n})}\exp\left(-s_{n-2}\sigma(v_{n-1})\right)\int_{0}^{s_{n-2}}\exp\left(s_{n-1}\left(\sigma(v_{n-1})-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{n-1}\\
&\leqslant\frac{2}{\sigma(v_{n})}\exp\left(-s_{n-2}\sigma(v_{n-1})\right)\frac{\exp\left(s_{n-2}\left(\sigma(v_{n-1})-\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right)-1}{\sigma(v_{n-1})-\frac{\delta}{2}}\end{split}

and, as before,

G_{2}(s_{n-2},v_{n-1},v_{n},w)\leqslant\frac{2}{\sigma(v_{n})}\frac{2}{\sigma(v_{n-1})}\exp\left(-s_{n-2}\frac{\delta}{2}\right)

Iterating this process (recalling that
s_{0}=t
, we end up with the following estimate for the integral over the simplex:

\int_{\Delta_{t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(-(s_{j-1}-s_{j})\sigma(v_{j})\right)\exp\left(-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}s_{n}\\
\leqslant\frac{2^{n}}{\prod_{j=1}^{n}\sigma(v_{j})}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\quad\forall(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\Lambda_{\delta}^{n},\quad w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}.

Inserting this into (
A.9
) yields

\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant 2^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\bm{k}(v_{j},w)}{\sigma(v_{j})}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{n})

and, using that

\sigma(v_{j})\geqslant\delta\qquad\text{ whereas }\int_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v_{j},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{j})\leqslant\int_{V}\bm{k}(v_{j},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{j})=\sigma(w)

for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
we obtain

(A.11)

\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)\leqslant\left(\frac{2\sigma(w)}{\delta}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\,,\qquad\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0,\delta>0,\qquad w\in\Lambda_{\frac{\delta}{2}}.

Inserting this, together with (
A.10
), into (
A.8
) gives

\|U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{V}\mathbf{1}_{\Sigma_{\frac{\delta}{2}}}|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
+\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\int_{V}\sigma(w)^{n}|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

Introducing as before
g(x,v)=\sigma^{-k}(v)f(x,v)
, we get

\|U_{n}^{(\delta)}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{k}\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\left(\frac{2\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}{\delta}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}

and, for
k=N_{0}
, using (
A.7
) we end up with

\|U_{n}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C(2^{n}-1)\,\delta^{N_{0}}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{N_{0}}\|g\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}+\left(\frac{2\|\sigma\|_{\infty}}{\delta}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\\
\leqslant C_{1}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}\left(\left(\frac{\delta}{2}\right)^{N_{0}}+\left(\frac{2}{\delta}\right)^{n}\exp\left(-t\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right),\qquad\forall\delta>0.

where
C_{1}
depends on
n
,
N_{0}
and
\|\sigma\|_{\infty}
but not on
\delta
. Picking now, for
t>e
,

\delta=2(n+N_{0})\frac{\log t}{t}

one gets easily the result as in Lemma
A.2
.
∎

We establish here the general estimate allowing the definition of
\Theta_{n}^{(\delta)}(t,w)
that has been used in the previous Lemma. We give a more general result which applies to general integral operator
\mathcal{K}
and not only to
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
. The version for
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
being deduced obviously by observing that the kernel of
\mathcal{K}^{(\delta)}
is
\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{\delta}}\bm{k}(v,w)
.

Lemma A.4
.

For

\mathcal{K}f(x,v)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)f(x,w)\mathrm{d}w

the norm of the Dyson-Phillips iterated
\left(U_{n}(t)\right)_{n\geqslant 0}
is such that

\left\|U_{n}(t)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\Theta_{n}(t,w)\,|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w),\qquad\forall t\geqslant 0,\qquad n\geqslant 0

for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
where

\Theta_{n}(t,w)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)\right)\Theta_{n-1}(s,w)\mathrm{d}s,\qquad n\geqslant 1

and
\Theta_{0}(s,w)=\exp\left(-s\sigma(w)\right).
In particular,

(A.12)

\Theta_{n}(t,w)=\int_{V}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\bm{k}(v_{j},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{n})\\
\int_{\Delta_{t}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left(-(s_{j-1}-s_{j})\sigma(v_{j})\right)\exp\left(-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}s_{n}.

where we used the convention
s_{0}=t
and
\Delta_{t}
denotes the simplex

\Delta_{t}=\left\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\;;\;0\leqslant s_{n}\leqslant s_{n-1}\leqslant\ldots s_{1}\leqslant t\right\}.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction. For
n=0
, the result is obvious. Assume the result to be true for some
n\geqslant 0
. Let us fix
t\geqslant 0,

f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Then, from

U_{n+1}(t)f=\int_{0}^{t}U_{0}(t-s)\mathcal{K}U_{n}(s)f\mathrm{d}s

one has, introducing
h_{n}(s,x,v)=U_{n}(s)f(x,v)
,

U_{n+1}(t)f(x,v)=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}s\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)h_{n}(s,x-(t-s)v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)

and, introducing the change of variable
y=x-(t-s)v
, we get easily

\|U_{n+1}f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-s)\sigma(v)}\mathrm{d}s\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)|h_{n}(s,y,w)|\bm{m}(d{v})\\
\leqslant\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)\right)\mathrm{d}s\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|h_{n}(s,y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\\
\leqslant\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)\right)\|U_{n}(s)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\mathrm{d}s

which, thanks to the induction hypothesis, gives

\|U_{n+1}(t)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,w)|\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s)\sigma(v)\right)\Theta_{n}(s,w)\mathrm{d}s

from which the desired estimate easily follows. This achieves the proof by induction. One shows then by direct inspection that

\Theta_{n}(t,w)=\int_{V}\bm{k}(v_{1},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{1})\int_{V}\bm{k}(v_{2},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{2})\ldots\int_{V}\bm{k}(v_{n},w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v_{n})\\
\int_{0}^{t}\exp\left(-(t-s_{1})\sigma(v)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{1}\int_{0}^{s_{1}}\exp\left(-(s_{1}-s_{2})\sigma(v_{1})\right)\mathrm{d}s_{2}\\
\times\ldots\int_{0}^{s_{n}}\exp\left(-(s_{n-1}-s_{n})\sigma(v_{n})-s_{n}\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}s_{n}

which gives (
A.12
).
∎

Combining Lemmas
2.1
and
A.3
one deduces easily Proposition
2.3

A.3.
Inverse Laplace transform

We establish here a somehow classical result regarding the inverse Laplace transform where we recall that
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)
has been defined in (
2.9
).

Proposition A.5
.

Under Assumption (
2.12
), for any
n\geqslant 5
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
,
one has

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f=\frac{\exp(\varepsilon t)}{2\pi}\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta,\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

for any
t>0
,
\varepsilon>0
where

(A.13)

\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)f:=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{k+1}f,\qquad\forall\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0,f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

Proof.

The fact that the Laplace transform of
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
is exactly
\mathscr{S}_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f
is a well-known fact and the complex Laplace inversion formula
[
2
, Theorem 4.2.21]
allows to directly deduce that
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
is the Cesarò limit of the family

\left(\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta\right)_{\ell}.

One has to work a little bit more to deduce that it is a
classical limit
.
We recall that

\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)U_{n}(t)\mathrm{d}t=\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{n}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n}.

With this in mind, for any
n\geqslant 0
and any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, it holds

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f\mathrm{d}t&=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\lambda t\right)U_{k+1}(t)f\mathrm{d}t\\
&=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{k+1}f=:\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)f,\qquad\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0\end{split}

where we recall that, for
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0
,
\|\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<1.
Since moreover, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, the mapping
t\geqslant 0\mapsto\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
is continuous and bounded, with
\bm{S}_{n+1}(0)f=0
, one applies the complex Laplace inversion formula
[
2
, Theorem 4.2.21]
to deduce

(A.14)

\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f=\frac{\exp(\varepsilon t)}{2\pi}\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{2L}\int_{-L}^{L}\mathrm{d}\ell\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta,\qquad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

for any
t>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, i.e.
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
is the Cesarò limit of the family

\left(\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left(i\eta t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta\right)_{\ell}.

Let us prove it is actually a classical limit.
Fix
\varepsilon>0
and
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Recalling the notation,

\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A}),\qquad\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0,

one has, for any
\mathrm{Re}\lambda>0

\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{k+1}=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{n+1}\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\lambda})

where we notice that, for
\lambda=\varepsilon+i\eta
,

\left\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta})\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}(1,\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<\infty\,.

Since
\sup_{\eta}\|\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
, we deduce that there exists
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\left\|\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{\varepsilon}\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{n+1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})},\qquad\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

For
n+1\geqslant\mathsf{p}
, one has
\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{n+1}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{\mathbb{X}0})}\leqslant\left\|\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}^{\mathsf{p}}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}
, we deduce from (
2.12
) that there is
M_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left\|\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta\leqslant M_{\varepsilon},\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0.

This of course implies that

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left\|\exp\left((\varepsilon+i\eta)t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\mathrm{d}\eta\leqslant M_{\varepsilon}\exp(\varepsilon t),\qquad\forall\varepsilon>0.

In particular, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, the limit

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\exp\left((\varepsilon+i\eta)t\right)\mathscr{S}_{n+1}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\mathrm{d}\eta

exists in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. Since its Cesarò limit is
\bm{S}_{n+1}(t)f
, we deduce the result.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of the operator
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}

We collect in this Appendix some technical results regarding the properties of
\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}
.

B.1.
Proof of Proposition
4.15

We given in this section the full proof of Proposition
4.15
which regards the norm of derivatives of

\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)=\mathsf{M}_{\lambda}^{n}=\left[\mathcal{K}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]^{n},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

We focus first one the convergence in
\mathbb{X}_{0}
. One checks easily by induction that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
and any
n\geqslant 1
,

\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\int_{V^{n}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\bm{k}(w,w_{2})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\times\\
\times\exp\left(-\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)f\left(x-\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}w_{j},w_{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{n},

for any
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}

It is clear then that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{p}

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{p}}\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)f(x,v)=\left(-1\right)^{p}\int_{V^{n-1}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\bm{k}(w,w_{2})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\times\\
\times\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}\,\exp\left(-\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)f\left(x-\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}w_{j},w_{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{n}.

Set then, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{p}
and any
(x,v)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V
,

(B.1)

\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f(x,v)=(-1)^{p}\int_{V^{n-1}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\bm{k}(w,w_{2})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\times\\
\times\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}\,f\left(x-\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}w_{j},w_{n}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{n}.

As before, one observes thanks to the change of variable
x\mapsto y=x-\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}w_{j}
that

(B.2)

\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w_{n})\right|\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n})\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})

where

\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n})=\int_{V^{n}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\times\\
\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t_{1}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t_{n}\,.

We recall the multinomial formula

\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}^{r_{j}}\,,

where
\bm{r}=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
is a multi-index with
|\bm{r}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}=p
and
{p\choose\bm{r}}=\frac{p!}{r_{1}!\ldots\,r_{n}!}.
With this, one easily sees that

\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t_{1}\right)&\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t_{n}\\
&=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\int_{[0,\infty)^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}^{r_{j}}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{j})t_{j}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\mathrm{d}t_{n}\\
&=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma(w_{j})}\right)^{r_{j}+1}.\end{split}

Therefore

\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n})=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\int_{V^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma(w_{j})}\right)^{r_{j}+1}\bm{k}(w_{j-1},w_{j})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{0})\,.

Let
\bm{r}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|\bm{r}|=p
be given. One sees that

\int_{V^{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma(w_{j})}\right)^{r_{j}+1}\bm{k}(w_{j-1},w_{j})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{0})\,\\
=\sigma(w_{n})^{-r_{n}-1}\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{1})^{-r_{1}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{2})^{-r_{2}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{2})\ldots\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\sigma(w_{n-1})^{-r_{n-1}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1}).

Using the definition of
\vartheta_{s}
in (
1.7
), one has, since
r_{j}\leqslant p\leqslant N_{0}
,

\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{1})^{-r_{1}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\\
=\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{1})^{-r_{1}}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})=\sigma(w_{2})\vartheta_{r_{1}}(w_{2})\leqslant\|\vartheta_{r_{1}}\|_{\infty}\sigma(w_{2}).

Computing then the integral with respect to
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{2})
and then to
\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{j})
for increasing
j\in\{2,\ldots,n-1\}
, one easily checks that

\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{1})^{-r_{1}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\\
\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{1},w_{2})\sigma(w_{2})^{-r_{2}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{2})\ldots\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\sigma(w_{n-1})^{-r_{n-1}-1}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\\
\leqslant\|\vartheta_{r_{1}}\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{r_{2}}\|_{\infty}\ldots\|\vartheta_{r_{n-2}}\|_{\infty}\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\sigma(w_{n-1})^{-r_{n-1}}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\\
\leqslant\|\vartheta_{r_{1}}\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{r_{2}}\|_{\infty}\ldots\|\vartheta_{r_{n-1}}\|_{\infty}\sigma(w_{n}).

Therefore,

\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n})\leqslant\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\sigma(w_{n})^{-r_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\|\vartheta_{r_{j}}\|_{\infty}

and

\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\|\vartheta_{r_{j}}\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,w_{n})|\,\sigma(w_{n})^{-r_{n}}\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n}).

This proves that

\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{n,p}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{p}}

with

C_{n,p}=\sum_{|\bm{r}|=p}{p\choose\bm{r}}\prod_{j=1}^{n-1}\|\vartheta_{r_{j}}\|_{\infty}.

As in the proof of the previous Lemma, this allows to prove by a simple use of the dominant convergence theorem that

\underset{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}{\lim_{\lambda\to 0}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{p}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{p}}\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)f-\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{p}
. This proves the convergence in
\mathbb{X}_{0}.
To deal with the convergence in
\mathbb{X}_{s}
, one simply notices from the above proof that

\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}^{(p)}(0)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}|f(y,w_{n})|\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,p,s}(w_{n})\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})

with now

\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,p,s}(w_{n})=\int_{V^{n}}\sigma(w_{0})^{-s}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{0})\times\\
\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t_{1}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}t_{j}\right)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t_{n}\,.

The only difference between
\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,p,s}(w_{n})
and
\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n})
lies in the additional weight
\sigma(w_{0})^{-s}
in the first integral. But, since

\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\sigma(w_{0})^{-s}\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{0})\leqslant\sigma(w_{1})\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}=\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\int_{V}\bm{k}(w_{0},w_{1})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{0})

one sees from the above proof that
\left|\widetilde{\Theta}_{n,p,s}(w_{n})\right|\leqslant\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\Theta_{n,p}(w_{n}).
This gives (
4.21
) and achieves the proof of Proposition
4.15
.

Remark B.1
.

Notice that the above computations actually apply to the case
without derivatives
(corresponding of course to
p=0
) and, from (
B.2
), one sees then that, for any
N\geqslant 1
,

\sup_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{s}}\leqslant\|\mathsf{L}_{n}(0)f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{1}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|f(y,w_{n})\right|\Theta_{n,0,s}(w_{n})\mathrm{d}y\,\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n})

where now

\Theta_{n,0,s}(w_{n})=\int_{V^{n}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})\ldots\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)}{\left(\min(1,\sigma(v)\right)^{s}}\times\\
\times\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{1})t_{1}\right)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\ldots\int_{0}^{\infty}\exp\left(-\sigma(w_{n})t_{n}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{\sigma(w_{n})}\int_{V^{n}}\bm{k}(v,w_{1})\ldots\bm{k}(w_{n-1},w_{n})\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{n-1})}{\sigma(w_{n-1})}\ldots\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w_{1})}{\sigma(w_{1})}\frac{\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)}{\left(\min(1,\sigma(v)\right)^{s}}.

One has then

\Theta_{n,0,s}(w_{n})\leqslant\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\qquad\forall w_{n}\in V,\qquad s\leqslant N_{0}.

Consequently,

(B.3)

\left\|\mathsf{L}_{n}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{s})}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{s}\|_{\infty}\qquad\forall s\leqslant N_{0}

We also establish the following convergence of derivatives of
\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}f

Lemma B.2
.

For any
k\geqslant 0
and any
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
one has

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\varphi-\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\varphi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0.

Proof.

For
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
one checks easily from (
4.9
) that

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\varphi(x,v)-\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\varphi(x,v)\\
=(-i)^{k}\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left[\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)-1\right]\exp\left(-\left(i\eta+\sigma(w)\right)t\right)\varphi(x-tw,w)\mathrm{d}t

from which we deduce easily that

\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\varphi-\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\varphi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}\times V}\left|\varphi(y,w)\right|\mathrm{d}y\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}w)\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\\
\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left[1-\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)\right]\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\,.

One has

\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left[1-\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)\right]\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t=k!\sigma(w)^{-k-1}

so that

\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left[1-\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)\right]\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t\leqslant k!\sigma(w)^{-k}.

Using that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{V}\bm{k}(v,w)\bm{m}(\mathrm{d}v)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k}\left[1-\exp\left(-\varepsilon t\right)\right]\exp\left(-t\sigma(w)\right)\mathrm{d}t=0\qquad\text{ for a.e. }w\in V

and, since
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{k}
, we deduce the result from the Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem.
∎

Remark B.3
.

Notice that, if
\varphi\in\mathbb{X}_{k+1}
, one sees that

\sup_{\eta\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{\varepsilon+i\eta}\varphi-\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\mathsf{M}_{i\eta}\varphi\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant\varepsilon\Gamma(k+2)\|\varphi\|_{\mathbb{X}_{k+1}}

making the above convergence quantitative.

B.2.
Additional properties

Introduce now the operators

\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)=\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{n},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+},\qquad n\in\mathbb{N}.

Notice that,
\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\in\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})
for any
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
since

\|\mathsf{G}_{1}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}

with

\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty},\qquad\text{ and }\quad\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 1.

Therefore,

\|\mathsf{G}_{1}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty},\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

It is striking to observe that
\|\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}
can actually be estimated
uniformly with respect to

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Namely,

Lemma B.4
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\,\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty}\qquad\forall\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}.

In particular, for any
n\in\mathbb{N},k\in\mathbb{N}

(B.4)

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{n+k}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\,\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{n}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

for any
\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}
.

Proof.

One simply observes that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathsf{L}_{n-1}(\lambda)\mathcal{K}

so that

\|\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\|\mathsf{L}_{n-1}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

where, as known (see (
B.3
)),

\|\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\leqslant 1,\qquad\|\mathsf{L}_{n-1}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{1})}\leqslant\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty},\qquad\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\|\sigma\|_{\infty}

which gives the result. To prove (
B.4
) one simply observes that

\|\mathsf{G}_{n+k}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}=\|\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\mathsf{G}_{k}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\mathsf{G}_{k}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

and the estimate follows from the first part of the proof.
∎

We notice also that

\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(k)}(\lambda)=\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}\mathsf{G}_{1}(\lambda)=\left[\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]\mathcal{K}

and, for
k\leqslant N_{0}-1
,

\left\|\left[\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]\mathcal{K}\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\left\|\left[\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\right]\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{k+1},\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathcal{K}\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0},\mathbb{X}_{k+1})}\leqslant k!\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{k}\|_{\infty}

where we used (
4.7
). Thus

(B.5)

\sup_{\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(k)}(\lambda)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant k!\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\,\|\vartheta_{k}\|_{\infty}.

This shows that, for any
f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
, the mapping

\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\mapsto\mathsf{G}_{1}(\lambda)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

is of class
\mathscr{C}^{N_{0}-1}
. This easily extends to
\mathsf{G}_{n}(\lambda)f
and, besides the mere estimates for
\mathsf{G}_{n}^{(j)}(\lambda)
, we also need to understand the decay of
\mathsf{G}_{n}^{(j)}(i\eta)f
as
|\eta|\to\infty
. We resort to do so to the following

Lemma B.5
.

For any
k\in\{1,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
, there exists
\bar{C}_{k}>0
such that

(B.6)

\left\|\mathsf{G}^{(k)}_{N}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\bar{C}_{k}N^{k}\,\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-k}{2^{k}}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\qquad\forall N\geqslant 2^{k}+k.

Proof.

The proof is based upon elementary but tedious computations. We notice first that, since
\mathsf{G}_{N}(i\eta)=\left(\mathsf{G}_{1}(i\eta)\right)^{N}
, one has for the first derivative:

\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(1)}(i\eta)=\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(1)}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)

According to (
B.5
)

\|\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{1}\|_{\infty}=C_{1}

so that

\begin{split}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(1)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}&\leqslant C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
&\leqslant 2C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}.\end{split}

Since
N-1-r\geqslant\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor
for any
0\leqslant r\leqslant\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor
, we get thanks to (
B.4
) that

(B.7)

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(1)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2C_{1}^{2}\,\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})},\qquad N-1\geqslant 2

which results in

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(1)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2{C_{1}}^{3}(N+1)\left\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

since
\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{1}
for any
r
and
\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor+1\leqslant N
. This proves the result for
k=1
. Now, for
k=2
and
N\geqslant 4
, one has

\mathsf{G}^{(2)}_{N}(i\eta)=\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{G}^{(1)}_{r}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(1)}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{N-r}(i\eta)+\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(2)}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\\
+\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(1)}(i\eta)\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta).

Using (
B.5
),

\|\mathsf{G}_{1}^{(2)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2\|\sigma\|_{\infty}\|\vartheta_{2}\|_{\infty}=:C_{2},

while we recall that
\|\mathsf{G}^{(1)}_{1}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{1},

so that

\|\mathsf{G}^{(2)}_{N}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\|\mathsf{G}^{(1)}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
+C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
+C_{2}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,,

and, as before,

\|\mathsf{G}^{(2)}_{N}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 4C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
+2C_{2}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,.

Now, according to (
B.7
)

\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2C_{1}^{2}\|\mathsf{L}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r_{1}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

and, since
\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor\geqslant\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor
for any
r\leqslant\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor
, invoking (
B.4
) again we deduce

\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2C_{1}^{3}\|\mathsf{L}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r_{1}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\

Thus,

4C_{1}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}^{(1)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
\leqslant 8C_{1}^{4}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1-r}{2}\rfloor}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{r_{1}}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,,

while, as in the proof of (
B.7
)

2C_{2}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\,\|\mathsf{G}_{N-1-r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B})(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
\leqslant 2C_{2}C_{1}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
\leqslant 2C_{2}C_{1}^{2}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}.

Consequently,

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(2)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant 2C_{1}^{2}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\times\\
\times\left(4C_{1}^{2}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{r_{1}=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r_{1}}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}+C_{2}\sum_{r=0}^{\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor}\|\mathsf{G}_{r}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\right).

This clearly gives the rough estimate (using
\lfloor\frac{N-2-r}{2}\rfloor+1\leqslant\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\rfloor+1\leqslant N
),

(B.8)

\left\|\mathsf{G}_{N}^{(2)}(i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{2})}\leqslant 2C_{1}^{2}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{N-2}{4}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\left(2C_{1}^{2}N^{2}+C_{2}C_{1}N\right)

and proves the result for
k=2
. By a tedious but simple induction argument, we deduce then the result for any
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
. ∎

We can prove now Lemma
7.1
given in Section
7
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. Recall that we defined, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathsf{G}_{k}(\lambda)=\left(\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right)^{k},\qquad\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}

and we denoted its derivatives of order
j
by
\mathsf{G}_{k}^{(j)}(\lambda)
. With this operator, we notices that

{s}_{n}(\lambda):=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\mathsf{G}_{k}(\lambda)\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})\ \qquad\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}

Computing derivatives with Leibniz rule we get, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

(B.9)

\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}s_{n}(\lambda)f=\sum_{m=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}k\\
j\end{array}\right)\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m}(\lambda)\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k-j}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f\right]

Now, as observed, if
f\in\mathbb{X}_{k-j+1}
, then
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k-j}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f\right]\in\mathbb{X}_{0}
(see (
4.7
)) so that (see Eq. (
B.6
))

\mathsf{G}^{(j)}_{m}(\lambda)\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k-j}}\left[\mathcal{R}(\lambda,\mathcal{A})f\right]\in\mathbb{X}_{0}.

This easily proves that the mapping

\lambda\in\overline{\mathbb{C}}_{+}\longmapsto s_{n}(\lambda)f\in\mathbb{X}_{0}

is of class
\mathscr{C}^{N_{0}-1}
with

\sup_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}_{+}}\left\|\frac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{k}}s_{n}(\lambda)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}\leqslant C_{k}\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}_{N_{0}}}

for some positive
C_{k}>0
depending only on
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}.
Let us now prove

(B.10)

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k}}\,s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

for any
k\in\{0,\ldots,N_{0}-1\}
which will also prove the fact that the mapping
\eta\mapsto s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f
belongs to
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
. Starting from (
B.9
), we notice that, for any
k\leqslant N_{0}-1
,
m\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
,
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, one easily see that, for any
R>0
,

\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,1]}\sup_{|\eta|>R}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(j)}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\leqslant\sup_{|\eta|>R}\|\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(j)}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}\\
\leqslant\bar{C}_{k}n^{k}\sup_{|\eta|>R}\|\mathsf{G}_{\lfloor\frac{n-k}{2^{k}}\rfloor}(i\eta)\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}

according to Lemma
B.5
. from which we easily deduce that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,1]}\sup_{|\eta|>R}\left\|\mathsf{G}_{m}^{(j)}(\varepsilon+i\eta)\right\|_{\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{X}_{0})}<\infty.

Combining this with the fact that

\lim_{|\eta|\to\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,1]}\left\|\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^{k-j}}{\mathrm{d}\eta^{k-j}}\mathcal{R}(\varepsilon+i\eta,\mathcal{A})f\right\|_{\mathbb{X}_{0}}=0

(see Proposition
4.1
and Lemma
4.5
), we deduce (
B.10
) from the representation (
B.9
). The fact that
s_{n}(\varepsilon+i\eta)f
converges to
s_{n}(i\eta)f
in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
is then deduced from (
B.9
) and the limits established in Proposition
4.1
which easily implies that
\mathsf{G}_{k}(\varepsilon+i\eta)
converges to
\mathsf{G}_{k}(i\eta):=\left[\mathcal{R}(i\eta,\mathcal{A})\mathcal{K}\right]^{k}
in
\mathscr{C}_{0}^{N_{0}-1}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{X}_{0})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, details are left to the reader.∎

Appendix C
About collectively power compact operators

We prove the following result which is likely to be well-known but we were not able to find in the literature.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and let
B\in\mathscr{B}(X)
be such that there is
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

B^{n}
is compact. Let
\nu_{0}
be a semi-simple eigenvalue of
B^{n}
and let
\mu_{0}\in\mathfrak{S}(B)
be such that

\mu_{0}^{n}=\nu_{0}.

Then,
\mu_{0}
is a semi-simple eigenvalue of
B
. Moreover, if
\nu_{0}
is simple then so is
\mu_{0}
and the corresponding spectral projection coincides.

Remark C.2
.

Notice that, under the assumption of Theorem
C.1
, since
\nu_{0}
is a simple eigenvalue of
B^{n}
, there exists only
one
eigenvalue
\mu_{0}
of
B
such that

\nu_{0}=\mu_{0}^{n}.

Proof.

Observe that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
,

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda^{n-1-j}B^{j}\right)\left(\lambda-B\right)=\lambda^{n}-B^{n}.

In particular, if
\lambda^{n}\notin\mathfrak{S}(B^{n})
then
\lambda\notin\mathfrak{S}(B)
and

(C.1)

\mathcal{R}(\lambda^{n},B^{n})\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda^{n-1-j}A^{j}=\mathcal{R}(\lambda,B).

Let us assume now
\mu_{0}^{n}=\nu_{0}
is semi-simple and let
P_{0}
be the spectral projection associated to
\nu_{0}
(and
B^{n}
), then (recall that
\nu_{0}
is a simple pole of the resolvent
\mathcal{R}(\cdot,B^{n})

P_{0}=\lim_{z\to\nu_{0}}\left(z-\nu_{0}\right)\mathcal{R}(z,B^{n})=\lim_{\lambda\to\mu_{0}}\left(\lambda^{n}-\mu_{0}^{n}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda^{n},B^{n}).

Writing, for
\lambda^{n}\neq\mu_{0}
,

\left(\lambda-\mu_{0}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda,B)=\frac{\lambda-\mu_{0}}{\lambda^{n}-\mu_{0}^{n}}\left(\lambda^{n}-\mu_{0}^{n}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda^{n},B^{n})\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\lambda^{n-1-j}B^{j}

and using that

\lim_{\lambda\to\mu_{0}}\frac{\lambda-\mu_{0}}{\lambda^{n}-\mu_{0}^{n}}=\frac{1}{n\mu_{0}^{n-1}},

we deduce that the limit

\lim_{\lambda\to\mu_{0}}\left(\lambda-\mu_{0}\right)\mathcal{R}(\lambda,B)

exists and is equal to

\frac{1}{n\mu_{0}^{n-1}}P_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mu_{0}^{n-1-j}B^{j}.

Therefore,
\mu_{0}
is a simple pole of
\mathcal{R}(\cdot,B)
and

\Pi_{0}=\frac{1}{n\mu_{0}^{n-1}}P_{0}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\mu_{0}^{n-1-j}B^{j}.

In particular,
\mathrm{Range}(\Pi_{0})\subset\mathrm{Range}(P_{0})
and
\dim\left(\mathrm{Range}(\Pi_{0})\right)\leqslant\dim\left(\mathrm{Range}(P_{0})\right).
In particular, if
\nu_{0}
is simple then so is
\mu_{0}
with

\mathrm{Range}(\Pi_{0})=\mathrm{Range}(P_{0}).

Let now
\psi\in X
be an eigenfunction of
B
associated to
\mu_{0}
and let
\psi^{*}\in X^{*}
be an eigenfunction of
B^{*}
associated to
\mu_{0}
with the normalisation

\langle\psi^{*},\psi\rangle=1.

Then,
\mathrm{Range}(P_{0})=\mathrm{Range}(\Pi_{0})=\mathrm{Span}(\psi)
and for any
h\in X
, there is
\alpha_{h}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Pi_{0}h=\alpha_{h}\psi.

One has then
\langle\psi^{*},\Pi_{0}h\rangle=\alpha_{h}
i.e.

\alpha_{h}=\langle\Pi_{0}^{*}\psi^{*},h\rangle=\langle\psi^{*},h\rangle

i.e.

\Pi_{0}h=\langle\psi^{*},h\rangle\,\psi.

In the same way,
P_{0}h=\langle\psi^{*},h\rangle\,\psi
and
\Pi_{0}=P_{0}.

∎
